AlphaGenome 2 predicts regulatory activity and
variant effects across cell states
Adam Gayoso1* Q, Anne Mottram1*, Alexander Karollus1*, Guido Novati1*, Pavol Drotár1*, John Reid1*, Laetitia
Meng-Papaxanthos1*, Adam Kosiorek1*, Eirini Arvaniti1*, Vincent Dutordoir1, Soham De1, Tom Ward1, Lai Hong
Wong1, Matteo Perino1, Kyle Taylor1, Jun Cheng1, Natasha Latysheva1, Joshua Pan1, Dhavanthi Hariharan1, Clare
Bycroft1, Lauren Nicolaisen1, Demis Hassabis1, Pushmeet Kohli1 Q and Žiga Avsec1 Q
1Google DeepMind
*Equal contribution
QCorresponding authors: adamgayoso@google.com, pushmeet-contact@google.com, avsec@google.com
Most genetic variants associated with human traits and diseases lie in non-coding DNA, where they can
alter gene regulation in specific cell types and states. Single-cell atlases chart these states across tissues
and development, but rarely profile enough donors to associate variants with expression directly. Deep
learning models could bridge this gap by predicting variant effects from DNA sequence, yet they poorly
distinguish related cell states and require fine-tuning for new datasets. Here we present AlphaGenome 2
(AG2), which substantially advances cell-type-specific prediction of regulatory activity and variant effects
for gene expression and chromatin accessibility. Trained on organism-wide human and mouse single-cell
atlases and bulk functional genomics data, AG2 takes as input a 1-Mb DNA sequence and a condition
specifying a target tissue, cell type, or cell state. Conditioning enables AG2 to impute unmeasured
epigenomic tracks and predict multimodal variant effects across cell types. Inferring this condition directly
from gene expression extends AG2 to unseen datasets and cell states without fine-tuning. AG2 outperforms
fine-tuned AlphaGenome 1 and specialized models trained on target datasets, doubling recall of causal
single-cell eQTLs at 90% precision. AG2 also achieves state-of-the-art enhancer–gene linking and can
pinpoint causal variants at complex trait loci. Across hundreds of human cell types, we generated an atlas
of enhancer–gene links and scores for 7 million common variants. As single-cell profiling expands across
development, tissues, and disease cohorts, AG2 provides a blueprint for turning observational atlases into
variant effect maps.
Introduction
A single human genome gives rise to hundreds of distinct cell types and states, whose different gene expression
programs are driven by selective utilization of non-coding regulatory sequences. Single-cell initiatives such as the
Human Cell Atlas1 and repositories such as CELLxGENE2 have rapidly cataloged transcriptional and chromatin
states across primary tissues, development, and disease3–6, but our understanding of how non-coding regulatory
variation affects these cell states has lagged behind. Observational single-cell atlases typically sample too few
donors to map genetic effects directly. Resolving variant effects through single-cell quantitative trait locus (QTL)
mapping requires larger cohorts, as recently exemplified by TenK10K7. Even at this scale, QTL studies do not
clearly separate causal variants from those in linkage disequilibrium (LD), lack statistical power for rare variants, and
remain largely restricted to few accessible tissues. These factors contribute to a systematic gap between molecular
QTLs and complex-trait loci from genome-wide association studies (GWAS)8. Experimental perturbations face
complementary constraints: massively parallel reporter assays (MPRAs) test sequences outside native chromatin
context9,10, whereas endogenous editing approaches scale to only a limited set of loci and tractable cell types11–13.
Deep learning sequence-to-function (S2F) models could complement these experimental constraints by
predicting regulatory activity and variant effects in different cell types directly from DNA sequence. We recently
described AlphaGenome14 (hereafter AlphaGenome 1, or AG1), a state-of-the-art unified S2F model that predicts
diverse molecular readouts at up to single-base resolution from a 1-Mb human or mouse DNA sequence. Yet like
earlier multitask S2F models15,16, AlphaGenome 1 relies predominantly on bulk tissues and cell lines, predicts a
fixed catalog of experimentally measured tracks, and struggles to distinguish closely related cell types. Recent
studies have trained S2F models directly on single-cell data17–21 or conditioned models on tissues, cell types, or
cell states21–24 to impute unmeasured modalities22,24 and generalize to unseen samples23,24. However, neither
single-cell training nor conditioning alone has been shown to substantially improve cell-type specificity or variant
effect prediction over multitask models such as Borzoi16 and AlphaGenome 1. Weak cell-type specificity across
training in turn constrains generalization to unseen cell types and states. Therefore, advances in both predictive

performance and data coverage are required to enable cell-type-resolved variant interpretation for single-cell
atlases.
Here we present AlphaGenome 2 (AG2), a conditional S2F model that advances both predictive performance
and coverage across cell types and states, predicting regulatory activity and variant effects from a 1-Mb human or
mouse DNA sequence. To improve performance across closely related cell types, AlphaGenome 2 represents a
specified tissue, cell line, cell type, or cell state (which we refer to as a condition) as an embedding that modulates
sequence representations throughout the network, starting from the earliest layers. We trained AlphaGenome 2
across bulk functional genomics data, cell-type-resolved DNA methylation, and organism-wide human and mouse
single-cell RNA-seq (scRNA-seq) and ATAC-seq (scATAC-seq) atlases. Together, early conditioning and expanded
training data substantially sharpen cell-type specificity relative to unconditioned multitask and late-conditioned
models, enabling AlphaGenome 2 to outperform fine-tuned AlphaGenome 1 and specialized single-cell models18,21
on expression QTL (eQTL) variant effect prediction and distal enhancer–gene (E2G) linking. To expand coverage
across modalities and unseen cell states, AlphaGenome 2 decouples conditions from output heads to impute
unmeasured epigenomic assays in primary cells, and introduces a post-training stage that aligns a single-cell
expression autoencoder trained on over 100 million human scRNA-seq profiles into the frozen sequence model’s
condition space. Inferring condition embeddings directly from scRNA-seq extends AlphaGenome 2 to newly
profiled cohorts and rare cell states without fine-tuning, outperforming models trained directly on those cohorts18,21.
Leveraging AlphaGenome 2, we predict lineage-specific distal E2G links genome-wide and score nearly 7 million
trait-associated and common variants to help annotate complex traits with putatively causal variants, target genes
with direction of effect, and affected cell states.
Results
Condition-specific sequence modeling
AlphaGenome 2 is a deep learning model that takes as input a 1-Mb DNA sequence, a target condition (tissue,
cell line, cell type, or cell state), and a species identifier (human or mouse) and predicts genomic tracks spanning
diverse molecular readouts (Fig. 1a–c). Built on a U-Net-style backbone25 similar to AG114, the model integrates
proximal and distal sequence context across the 1-Mb window (Fig. 1b). To capture condition-specific regulation,
adaptive normalization layers26 modulate intermediate sequence representations using a condition embedding,
which is either selected from a learned dictionary of ontology-defined reference conditions or generated from
scRNA-seq profiles via a cell encoder (Fig. 1a; Methods). For a given condition, these predictions take the form of
base- and bin-resolution tracks (spanning RNA coverage, transcription initiation (CAGE), RNA splicing, chromatin
accessibility, histone modifications, transcription factor (TF) binding, and CpG methylation), scalar gene expression
values, and pairwise interactions representing chromatin contact maps and splice junctions (Fig. 1c).
AG2 was trained in four stages (pre-training, condition refinement, distillation, and post-training) on the
reference human and mouse genomes and an expansive corpus of bulk and single-cell genomics data (Fig. 1d;
Supplementary Fig. 1; Methods). Compared to AG1, which was trained exclusively on bulk assays, AG2 introduces
3977 single-cell RNA-seq and ATAC-seq pseudobulk tracks across 36 human and mouse studies (many of which
featured profiling of both modalities; Supplementary Table 1), adds cell-type-resolved CpG methylation spanning
185 human reference conditions28,29, and post-trains on single cells sampled from 107 million human scRNA-seq
profiles (Supplementary Tables 2 and 3). Across this corpus, bulk assays and cell-type-aggregated single-cell
pseudobulks are indexed by standard ontology terms into a fixed catalog of reference conditions, each representing
a distinct human or mouse tissue, cell line, or cell type and grouping all modalities measured for that condition
(Methods). Eight randomly initialized AG2 models were first pre-trained across these reference conditions and
then refined by further optimizing their reference condition embeddings and modulation layers with the rest of the
model frozen, yielding eight “teacher” models. For distillation, a single AG2 “student” model sharing the same
architecture was trained to reproduce the ensemble-averaged predictions of the teachers on randomly mutated
input sequences, transferring the robustness and variant effect prediction accuracy of the teacher ensemble into
a single model14,30. Finally, the student model was post-trained at the single-cell level on human scRNA-seq
profiles from CELLxGENE2 and our human single-cell pre-training corpus, freezing the distilled model and mapping
embeddings from a separately trained single-cell expression autoencoder into AG2’s condition space.
Because conditions are decoupled from shared modality heads and the distilled student backbone remains
frozen during post-training, a single AG2 model operates across both reference and expression conditioning. This
design supports cross-modality assay imputation in sparsely profiled conditions, adaptation to unseen scRNA-seq
cohorts without retraining, and variant effect scoring and sequence attribution across modalities (Fig. 1e). Across
our benchmarks, we evaluate dedicated models trained with genomic intervals, experimental tracks, or single-cell
datasets held out (Methods), while applying the full-genome AG2 model across hundreds of human conditions to
construct precomputed atlases of distal E2G links and GWAS variant effect predictions (Fig. 1f).

Figure 1 | AlphaGenome 2 adapts sequence representations to each condition to predict multimodal genomic readouts in observed
and unseen cell states. (a) AG2 takes as input a 1-Mb DNA sequence, a target condition, and species identifier (human or mouse).
Biological conditioning is specified either from a predetermined set of discrete reference conditions or from single-cell gene expression data for
generalization to unseen primary cell types and samples (Methods). (b) Biological and species conditioning occurs throughout the architecture of
AG2, modulating sequence representations across the U-Net encoder, central transformer tower, and decoder. For each condition, task-specific
modality heads predict both experimentally observed modalities and imputed modalities, which span coverage tracks, scalar gene-level
expression, and pairwise chromatin interactions. (c) For each condition, AG2 predicts transcription factor (TF) binding (128-bp resolution),
histone modifications (128 bp), chromatin accessibility (ATAC-seq and DNase-seq, 1 bp), CpG DNA methylation (1 bp), chromatin contact maps
(2048 bp), gene expression (1-bp stranded CAGE and RNA-seq coverage and scalar RNA-seq gene-level counts), and RNA splicing (1-bp
splice site location, usage, and splice junctions). (d) AG2 extends the AG1 bulk training corpus with single-cell RNA-seq, single-cell ATAC-seq,
and CpG methylation datasets. Additionally, its single-cell expression encoder was trained on over 100 million individual scRNA-seq profiles
across 68 tissues. (e) Capabilities of the model include cell-type-resolved variant effect prediction (VEP; comparing reference and alternative
allele profiles), nucleotide-resolution sequence attribution via in silico mutagenesis (ISM), zero-shot generalization to unseen conditions, and
cross-modality epigenomic imputation. (f) Precomputed predictions accompanying release include an enhancer–gene resource (genome-wide
predictions across 640 conditions) and a GWAS-centric variant effect resource (covering 7 million variants including Open Targets Platform27
single-nucleotide polymorphisms (SNPs) and common variants, across 510 conditions).
Improved condition-specific predictions
Accurately resolving differences in gene expression and chromatin accessibility across tissues and cell types
remains a major challenge for S2F models31–33, and at the single-cell level this difficulty is compounded by the
technical noise of single-cell assays. To evaluate AG2’s capacity to address these limitations, we assessed
predictions across the set of reference biological conditions using a teacher model trained with test genome
intervals held out. We constructed a directly comparable baseline (AG1-sc) by fine-tuning AG1 on the single-cell
reference conditions in our training corpus using the same genome split (Methods). Because AG1 outperforms
Borzoi at predicting condition-specific gene expression14, AG1-sc also provides a strong baseline relative to
Borzoi-based single-cell models.
We first evaluated the prediction of gene expression log fold changes (LFCs), computed per gene relative to
the mean across bulk tissues or across pseudobulked cell types within individual tissues. This approach isolates
condition-specific variation from overall expression level (Methods). Across GTEx tissues34, AG2 outperformed
AG1, yielding a median per-tissue Pearson correlation of 𝑟= 0.71 (vs. 0.61) and explaining 53% of cross-tissue
expression variance relative to the tissue mean (41%) (Fig. 2a). We observed comparable improvements on
pseudobulk scRNA-seq data across human and mouse tissues, where AG2 explained a median of 34% of within-
tissue cell-type-specific expression variance across comparison groups, compared with 23% for AG1-sc (Fig. 2b,
Supplementary Fig. 3a). Extending this analysis to LFCs of single-cell chromatin accessibility signal across
regulatory peaks, AG2 similarly captured a larger fraction of cell-type-specific variance (median 60% vs. 51%;
Fig. 2b, Supplementary Fig. 3a).
These improvements were evident at individual loci on held-out sequences. For the mouse gene Trpm5,
which is required for chemosensing and is selectively expressed in intestinal tuft cells35, AG2 accurately predicted
selective Trpm5 expression and tuft-cell-specific accessible chromatin peaks at the Trpm5 locus across mouse

cell types, whereas AG1-sc failed to resolve either signal (Supplementary Fig. 3b). Similarly, across human
hematopoietic differentiation, AG2 recovered continuous gene expression trajectories on held-out sequences for
key lineage-defining regulators from hematopoietic stem cells toward erythroid and B-cell lineages with higher
fidelity than AG1-sc (Supplementary Fig. 3c).
We ablated AG2 to determine whether these improvements stemmed from the expanded training data or the
architectural differences relative to AG1 (Supplementary Fig. 4a,b). On the same genome split, we compared
pre-trained AG2 models against an unconditioned AG1 multitask model retrained on matched bulk datasets and
against restricted-conditioning AG2 configurations pre-trained on the identical full corpus (Methods). Pre-trained
AG2 consistently improved gene expression LFC prediction across cell lines and GTEx tissues and condition-
specific peak prediction for histone and TF ChIP-seq (Supplementary Fig. 4b). Restricting where condition
embeddings enter the network showed that conditioning only at the output or decoder layers yielded minimal gains
over the multitask baseline, whereas conditioning the transformer and encoder drove the primary improvements
(Supplementary Fig. 4a,b).
Improved prediction of non-coding variant effects
We next evaluated whether improved condition-specific track modeling translates to more accurate prediction
of genetic variant effects using fine-mapped QTLs on held-out test chromosomes, scoring variants with the full-
genome distilled AG2 model (Methods). Across benchmarks, we assessed whether models could distinguish
putatively causal fine-mapped variants from matched negative controls (causality task) and predict the direction
(sign task) and magnitude (coefficient task) of allelic effects.
On bulk tissue eQTLs from GTEx across 49 tissues, AG2 improved prediction of allelic effect direction
and magnitude as well as prioritization of putatively causal variants compared with AG1 and Borzoi16 (Fig. 2c,
Supplementary Fig. 5a). These gains were concentrated in the high-precision regime most relevant for variant
prioritization: for causality prediction, AG2 increased recall at 90% precision from 17% for AG1 to 26% (overall
area under the precision–recall curve, AUPRC, 0.74 vs. 0.72 for AG1 and 0.69 for Borzoi), and for sign prediction, it
increased recall at 90% accuracy from 32% to 40% (overall area under the sign accuracy–recall curve, AUC, 0.85
vs. 0.83 and 0.77; Fig. 2c). Stratifying variants revealed that AG2’s improvement over AG1 was driven primarily by
eQTLs with putative tissue-specific effects (Fig. 2d; Methods), consistent with improved prediction of cross-tissue
expression differences carrying over to tissue-specific variant effects. Across the remaining bulk splicing, chromatin
accessibility, DNase sensitivity, and polyadenylation QTL benchmarks from AG114, AG2 performed comparably to
AG1 (Supplementary Fig. 2).
To evaluate variant effect prediction at the cell-type level, we benchmarked models on single-cell QTL from
two studies: TenK10K, which mapped variant effects on expression (sc-eQTLs; 28 cell types) and chromatin
accessibility (sc-caQTLs; 24 cell types) across peripheral blood mononuclear cells7,37, and IBDverse, which mapped
sc-eQTL in intestinal epithelial and immune cells (47 cell types, excluding blood)38,39. Both cohorts are scored using
matched reference conditions from our training corpus (Methods). Across the causality, sign, and coefficient tasks
constructed from fine-mapped variants, AG2 outperformed baseline models on all sc-eQTL tasks and sc-caQTL
tasks (Fig. 2e, Supplementary Fig. 5b,c,d). Gains were again largest in the 90% precision and accuracy regime:
AG2 increased recall from 16% for AG1-sc to 30% for TenK10K sc-eQTL causality (overall AUPRC 0.76 vs. 0.72 for
AG1-sc and 0.69 for Scooby), from 23% to 43% for TenK10K sc-eQTL sign prediction (overall AUC 0.85 vs. 0.79
and 0.77), and from 31% to 45% for IBDverse sc-eQTL sign (overall AUC 0.86 vs. 0.81; Fig. 2e). A full-genome
distilled AG2 model trained with all bulk PBMC tracks held out (“AG2 (no bulk PBMC)”; Methods) showed largely
unchanged single-cell QTL performance on TenK10K, with a small decrease in effect-size correlation (Spearman 𝜌
0.47 to 0.45; Supplementary Fig. 5e), demonstrating that these predictions do not depend on bulk PBMC training
data.
Previous evaluations of S2F models observed a steep decline in eQTL prediction accuracy with distance from
the transcription start site (TSS) and concluded that such models largely fail to capture distal regulatory elements33.
However, stratifying fine-mapped eQTLs by whether they overlap accessible chromatin reveals a sharp distinction
between in-peak and outside-peak variants that is masked in unstratified analyses (Supplementary Fig. 6a,b,c). For
TenK10K sc-eQTLs located within accessible primary blood scATAC-seq peaks, predictive performance remained
strong even at longer distances (>35 kb from the TSS); at these distances AG2 retained a clear advantage over
AG1-sc and Scooby in sign prediction (area under the receiver operating characteristic curve, AUROC, 0.86 vs.
0.78 for AG1-sc) and effect-size prediction (Spearman 𝜌0.57 vs. 0.47; Supplementary Fig. 6a). By contrast, outside
accessible peaks, AG1-sc and Scooby predicted sign at close to random chance level, and AG2 retained only
modest accuracy (sign AUROC up to 0.63; Supplementary Fig. 5e). Repeating this stratification on GTEx bulk
eQTLs using ENCODE candidate cis-regulatory elements (cCREs)40 as a proxy for accessible chromatin showed a
consistent but weaker difference between in-cCRE and outside-cCRE variants (Supplementary Fig. 6b), indicating
that S2F models can capture distal regulatory variant effects when variants reside within active regulatory elements.
Stratifying IBDverse sc-eQTL within proxy peaks (Methods), yielded similar results (Supplementary Fig. 6c).

Figure 2 | AlphaGenome 2 improves condition-specific gene expression and regulatory variant effect prediction across tissues and
primary cell types. (a) Bulk tissue gene expression prediction on held-out genomic intervals across GTEx tissues (𝑛= 52 tissues; 1885 genes)
for AlphaGenome 1 (AG1) and AlphaGenome 2 (AG2). Left: Per-tissue Pearson correlation (𝑟) between observed and predicted cross-tissue
gene log fold changes (LFCs). Right: Percentage of cross-tissue gene expression variation explained. Box plots (left) display the median (center
line), the 25th and 75th percentiles (box bounds), and the whiskers extend to 1.5 times the interquartile range from the box bounds; all data
points are overlaid. (b) Percentage of within-tissue cross-cell-type variation explained on held-out genomic intervals by AG1 fine-tuned on
single-cell reference conditions (AG1-sc) and AG2 for single-cell gene expression (left) and single-cell chromatin accessibility peaks (right).
Each point represents a tissue/dataset comparison group (Methods). Left (right): 𝑛= 51 (27) groups, comprising 11 (10) mouse and 40 (17)
human. Box plots are defined as in a. (c) GTEx v11 bulk tissue eQTL evaluation across 49 tissues comparing Borzoi, AG1, and AG2. Left:
Tissue-pooled precision–recall curves for distinguishing fine-mapped eQTLs (𝑛= 33,837 unique variant–tissue–gene combinations) from
distance-matched negative controls at a 1:1 ratio (causality; AUPRC in legend). Right: Cumulative sign accuracy versus recall (sign; area
under curve in legend, 51.3% positive, 𝑛= 33,829). Point callouts indicate recall at 90% precision (left) or 90% sign accuracy (right). (d)
Tissue-weighted mean AUROC for GTEx v11 eQTL sign prediction stratified into tissue-specific versus nonspecific eQTLs (Methods; 𝑛= 7169
specific and 𝑛= 26,660 nonspecific eQTLs). (e) Single-cell QTL evaluation across primary peripheral blood immune (TenK10K) and intestinal
(IBDverse) cell types. Left and middle: Cell-type-pooled precision–recall (causality, 1:1 negative-to-positive ratio) and sign accuracy–recall (sign,
47.8% positive) curves for 6346 causal sc-eQTLs across 28 cell types, comparing Scooby, AG1-sc, and AG2. Right: Sign accuracy–recall
(sign, 48.5% positive) curves for 3524 causal sc-eQTLs across 47 cell types. Point callouts indicate recall at 90% precision or 90% sign
accuracy. (f) Opposing cell-type-specific effects of rs62420789 on IL20RA. Top: Observed and AG2-predicted tissue-level eQTL scores across
seven GTEx tissues (left), AG2 cell-type-level variant scores across constituent lineages (right), and expression of GRHL2 and ZEB1 (bottom
rows). Bottom: In silico mutagenesis (ISM) attribution logos (variant shaded in orange) with matched reference motifs (right) in lung goblet
cells (activating GRHL2 motif, JASPAR36 MA1105.3) and lung blood vessel endothelial cells (repressive ZEB1 motif, JASPAR MA0103.4). (g)
Multimodal interpretation of rs8052244 (chr16:87368655:G>C) in CD14+ monocytes at the FBXO31 locus. Top: Predicted reference (REF)
and alternative (ALT) allele profiles for local scATAC-seq and bulk FBXO31 RNA-seq coverage conditioned on the CD14+ monocyte reference
condition. Bottom: ISM attribution logos for scATAC-seq accessibility and single-cell FBXO31 gene expression with matched motifs (right),
showing disruption of an ETS motif (orange shading, JASPAR SPI1 MA0080.5) adjacent to a repressive homeodomain motif (JASPAR HHEX
MA0183.1). (h) Cell-type prioritization for rare non-coding variants in congenital hyperinsulinism. Top: HK1 locus showing the intronic regulatory
region containing 𝑛= 6 disease-associated de novo variants and 𝑛= 80 common variants. Bottom: Box plots of AG2-predicted variant effects
(log fold change) on HK1 expression for the disease-associated and common variants across seven pancreatic and neuroendocrine primary cell
types (darker colors highlight causal 𝛽-cell conditions). Box plots are defined as in a.

To illustrate how cell-type-resolved predictions clarify bulk tissue associations, we examined the fine-mapped
GTEx eQTL rs62420789 (chr6:137026715:G>A), which increases IL20RA expression in lung and adipose
tissue but decreases its expression in esophagus mucosa and other tissues (Fig. 2f). AG2 resolved these opposing
tissue-level effects into distinct cell-type-specific mechanisms: it predicted that rs62420789 decreases IL20RA
expression in epithelial cells (such as lung goblet cells), where nucleotide-resolution in silico mutagenesis (ISM)
indicated disruption of an activating GRHL2 binding motif, but increases expression in endothelial cells (such as
lung blood vessel endothelial cells), where ISM indicated disruption of a repressive ZEB1 motif (Fig. 2f). Consistent
with these motif attributions, GRHL2 is selectively expressed in epithelial lineages, where ZEB1 is selectively
absent (Fig. 2f). Because bulk tissue RNA-seq measures the average expression across a mixture of constituent
cell types, a variant that acts in opposite directions in epithelial and endothelial cells can yield opposite bulk eQTL
signs depending on which cell population dominates a given tissue sample, explaining the apparent tissue-specific
reversal across GTEx tissues as a consequence of cellular composition.
Unlike models trained solely on chromatin accessibility, AG2 jointly models chromatin and gene expression to
capture variant effects across both layers of regulation simultaneously, even when they act in opposite directions.
For example, the fine-mapped QTL rs8052244 (chr16:87368655:G>C) in CD14+ monocytes is associated with
decreased local chromatin accessibility but increased expression of FBXO31 (Fig. 2g). AG2 recapitulated both
effects, and ISM attribution across the two modalities suggested that the variant disrupts an ETS-like motif that
establishes local accessibility, whereas this motif, together with an adjacent homeodomain motif, is predicted to
drive repression of FBXO31 (Fig. 2g). Independent macrophage QTL data41 further show that the alternate allele
is associated with reduced levels of the repressive mark H3K27me3 along the FBXO31 gene body, consistent with
a repressive role for this element.
Finally, we tested whether AG2 can retrospectively prioritize known disease-associated variants and their
relevant cell type in a published rare disease locus. In congenital hyperinsulinism, rare de novo non-coding variants
(𝑛= 6) within an intronic regulatory region of HK1 cause inappropriate insulin secretion by selectively upregulating
HK1 in pancreatic 𝛽cells42 (Fig. 2h). AG2 predicted strong gain-of-expression effects for these individual variants
specifically in pancreatic islet 𝛽cells (islet of Langerhans beta and pancreas beta reference conditions), while
predicting near-zero effects in non-endocrine pancreatic lineages, off-target endocrine cells, and 𝑛= 80 common
population variants within the surrounding regulatory region (Fig. 2h). Together, these results show that AG2
can accurately resolve regulatory variant effects and their underlying mechanisms across both bulk tissues and
individual cell types.
Imputing unmeasured epigenomic assays
Chromatin accessibility and histone modifications mark active and repressed regulatory elements across cell types,
yet profiling them across hundreds of human tissues and primary cell types is impractical due to cost and sample
constraints. As a result, even the largest epigenomic compendia remain incomplete, missing tens of thousands
of possible experiments43. This gap is widest in single-cell atlases, where most studies profile only scRNA-seq
because parallel or joint scATAC-seq is costly. Histone modifications are rarer still, as bulk ChIP-seq requires
purified cell populations and single-cell assays capture only one to a few marks per experiment with sparse per-cell
coverage and specialized protocols44. Because AG2 was trained on bulk and single-cell data together, every
assay measured in a condition informs a shared condition embedding. Thus, in principle, the model can impute
unmeasured modalities from any combination of observed assays and predict variant effects in unmeasured assays
(Fig. 1b; Methods).
To benchmark imputation performance, we trained a full-genome distilled AG2 model (the imputation-split model;
Methods) holding out a test set of 184 bulk tracks across 30 conditions, spanning chromatin accessibility, RNA-seq,
and ChIP-seq for both TFs and histone modifications (Supplementary Fig. 7a, Supplementary Table 4). The other
assays measured in each condition remained in training, reflecting the common situation in which a condition
has been profiled with some assays but not others. For a consistent comparison against a baseline method, we
retrained Avocado45, a tensor factorization method, on the same split after a comprehensive hyperparameter
search (Methods). We also compared performance against two baselines that copy signal from training tracks of the
same assay: their mean, and an oracle that uses the held-out track to select and average the five most correlated
training tracks (closest), thereby approximating an upper bound for transferring signal from similar conditions
(Methods). Because most signal is shared across conditions, genome-wide correlation with the observed track
largely reflects this shared signal: the mean, which contains no condition-specific information, reached Pearson 𝑟of
0.47–0.72 across chromatin assays, similar to Avocado (0.39–0.73; Supplementary Fig. 8f). Across the consensus
peak catalog for each assay, we therefore scored each region by its mean predicted coverage and evaluated how
accurately this classified whether the region carried a called peak in the held-out condition (peak AUPRC; Methods).
Peaks shared by most conditions can be predicted by averaging other conditions, so we also stratified consensus
peaks by the fraction of conditions in which they are called. For RNA-seq, whose signal is likewise dominated by
genes expressed in all conditions, we compared predicted and observed expression differences between held-out
6

conditions (gene log fold changes; Methods).
AG2 recovered held-out peaks more accurately than all three alternatives for each chromatin assay (peak
AUPRC of 0.69–0.79; Supplementary Fig. 7b), better predicted expression differences between conditions for
RNA-seq (Pearson 𝑟= 0.74, compared with 0.58 for Avocado and 0.69 for closest; Supplementary Fig. 7b), and
outperformed Avocado on 166 of 181 evaluated tracks (Supplementary Fig. 8a). Gains were largest on the rarest
peaks (≤12.5% of conditions), where AG2 achieved a 1.7- to 2.9-fold higher AUPRC than the better of Avocado and
closest, and negligible on peaks shared across nearly all conditions (Supplementary Fig. 8b–e). For TF ChIP-seq
(66 held-out tracks across 43 factors), both Avocado and closest performed near or below random on these rare
peaks (Supplementary Fig. 8b). These results are consistent with AG2 inferring condition-specific peaks from DNA
sequence rather than copying signal from related conditions, as illustrated by held-out H3K4me1 enhancer peaks
at the LMO1 locus in SK-N-SH cells (Supplementary Fig. 7c). We note that each held-out TF track comes from a
condition in which ChIP-seq for that factor was performed, and thus in which the factor is expressed; we expect
that imputing binding in conditions where a factor is not expressed or otherwise inactive will yield poor performance
with false-positive peaks due to a lack of negative feedback provided to the model.
We then tested whether imputation can annotate enhancers in primary human immune cells profiled only with
single-cell assays. Using the imputation-split model, which also held out all bulk PBMC and astrocyte tracks, we
imputed H3K27ac across nine PBMC conditions with observed scRNA-seq and scATAC-seq and combined it with
observed accessibility to classify open non-promoter representative DNase I hypersensitive sites (rDHSs) with
enhancer-like signatures (Methods). Compared with held-out primary PBMC Mint-ChIP H3K27ac profiles46,47,
imputed H3K27ac identified enhancer-like elements more accurately across all nine cell types (AUPRC 0.89–0.98)
than either observed primary scATAC-seq alone (0.62–0.83) or observed H3K27ac ChIP-seq from lineage-matched
cell lines (0.76–0.93; Supplementary Fig. 7d), and predicted cell-type differences in H3K27ac across genome-wide
rDHSs (𝑟= 0.44; Supplementary Fig. 7e). Thus, imputing H3K27ac from single-cell accessibility and expression
can help annotate enhancers in primary cell types.
Generalizing to new cell states
Fixed-track S2F models such as AG1 and CREsted18 must be trained or fine-tuned for new datasets and cell states,
whereas cell-conditioned models such as Scooby21 are trained on individual datasets and do not generalize across
all cell types. To generalize well to new samples across human biology without additional training, we introduced a
post-training stage that derives AG2 conditions from scRNA-seq profiles (Fig. 3a; Methods). We first trained an
scVI-inspired single-cell expression autoencoder51 on human scRNA-seq data largely sourced from CELLxGENE2
(Methods). We then post-trained the distilled AG2 student model to predict each cell’s autoencoder reconstruction
from DNA sequence, conditioned on that cell’s embedding. Because these reconstructions are denoised estimates
of expression rather than sparse counts, they serve as pseudobulk-like targets at single-cell resolution, exposing
AG2 to the full diversity of profiled human cell states without collapsing cells into discrete pseudobulks. At inference,
rather than running the sequence model on every cell, we condition AG2 on a cell population (such as a cell type or
metacell) by encoding its cells and averaging their embeddings (Fig. 3d; Methods).
We first evaluated two aspects of the autoencoder: its cell embeddings and its expression reconstructions. To
test the cell embeddings on unseen data, we applied the encoder to PBMC scRNA-seq profiles from a SARS-CoV-2
vaccination study48; without dataset-specific training, the embeddings preserved cell-type structure on par with an
scVI model trained on this dataset, with similar integration benchmarking scores in this cohort with limited batch
effects52 (Supplementary Fig. 9a,b). To test the reconstructions in the realistic setting of newly generated studies,
we used a temporal split of CELLxGENE and evaluated across 16 held-out test datasets (Methods). For each cell
type, we averaged the embeddings of 𝑘cells and decoded this mean embedding into a single expression profile
(Supplementary Fig. 9c). Decoding a single cell’s embedding (𝑘= 1) already recovered observed pseudobulk gene
LFCs across cell types (median Pearson 𝑟= 0.61), confirming that single-cell reconstructions provide effective
pseudobulk-like targets for post-training. Moreover, although the autoencoder was trained on individual cells,
decoding the mean embedding of an increasing number of cells improved agreement with observed pseudobulk
LFCs up to a median 𝑟of 0.87 across all cells (Supplementary Fig. 9c), suggesting that the embedding space
is locally linear and demonstrating that averaging cell embeddings denoises individual cells to represent a cell
population at inference.
We next asked whether expression conditioning could replace dataset-specific fine-tuning. Starting from a
distilled student model trained with genomic intervals held out (and a cell encoder trained without access to
genes in those intervals), we compared post-training against fine-tuning the same student model on pseudobulk
expression from each CELLxGENE dataset (Methods). On the held-out intervals, expression conditioning predicted
cell-type-specific expression more accurately than fine-tuning across 147 evaluation groups of related cell types
from training-split datasets (𝑃= 3.1 × 10−12; one-sided paired Wilcoxon signed-rank test; Fig. 3b) and across 20
evaluation groups from test-split datasets unseen during autoencoder training and post-training (𝑃= 1.6 × 10−4;
one-sided paired Wilcoxon signed-rank test; Fig. 3c), demonstrating that a cell representation learned across the

Figure 3 | Expression conditioning extends AlphaGenome 2 to unseen single-cell datasets and cell states. (a) Post-training procedure
(Methods). A pre-trained scRNA-seq autoencoder embeds single cells and reconstructs denoised gene expression. With the autoencoder
and distilled AG2 frozen, a trainable adapter maps cell embeddings into AG2’s condition space and a trainable head predicts each cell’s
reconstructed expression from DNA sequence. (b,c) Cell-type-specific gene expression prediction on held-out genomic intervals comparing
post-trained and dataset-fine-tuned AG2 across CELLxGENE evaluation groups from the training split (b; 𝑛= 147) and a chronologically
held-out test split (c; 𝑛= 20). Each point shows the mean cross-gene Pearson 𝑟between predicted and observed LFCs (per cell type relative
to the mean) within 5–10 cell types from the same lineage, tissue, and dataset (Methods; dashed line, 𝑦= 𝑥; one-sided paired Wilcoxon
signed-rank test). (d) Expression-conditioning workflow on unseen studies. Single cells from post-vaccination PBMCs48 and from substantia
nigra and cingulate cortex in a Parkinson’s disease cohort49 are embedded with the cell encoder and averaged per cell type or metacell to
impute chromatin accessibility and score variant effects on accessibility and expression. (e–h) SARS-CoV-2 vaccination PBMC cohort (days 11
and 28 pooled; AG2 conditioned on scRNA-seq, observed accessibility from separate scATAC-seq cells; Methods)48. (e) Predicted versus
observed scATAC-seq LFC between vaccine-induced group A CD8+ T (VI-A CD8+ T) and effector memory CD8+ T (CD8 TEM) cells across
predicted accessible peaks (𝑛= 37,035; Pearson 𝑟= 0.46; orange circle, peak containing rs13066062; dashed line, 𝑦= 𝑥). (f) Left: Observed
(Obs.) and imputed (Imp.) scATAC-seq profiles (independently min–max scaled across states) in naive CD8+ T (CD8 Naive), CD8 TEM, and
VI-A CD8+ T cells around rs13066062 (chr3:45976852:G>A, orange line) at FYCO1. Top right: Observed expression of TBX21 and EOMES
(log(CPM + 1); CPM, counts per million). Bottom right: scATAC-seq ISM logo around rs13066062 (orange shading) in VI-A CD8+ T cells with
the matched TBX21 motif (JASPAR MA0690.1). (g,h) Predicted effect of rs13066062 on local scATAC-seq (g) and CXCR6 expression (h)
across 881 PBMC metacells conditioned on their mean cell embeddings, shown on a uniform manifold approximation and projection (UMAP)50
of cell-encoder embeddings (cells colored by metacell score; outlined circles, metacells; inset in h, VI-A CD8+ T metacells). (i,j) Variant effect
direction across brain regions and cell types in the Parkinson’s disease cohort49 (Methods). (i) Sign accuracy versus recall across concordant
chromatin accessibility QTL and allele-specific chromatin accessibility variants (caQTL-ASCA; 𝑛= 86,596) for expression-conditioned AG2 and
cohort-trained CREsted. (j) Recall of overlapping eQTLs (𝑛= 1620) matched at the 87.3% sign accuracy achieved by cohort-fine-tuned Scooby
at its |LFC| > 0.05 reporting threshold.
CELLxGENE corpus shares information across datasets and cell states more effectively than fitting each dataset
separately. Post-training this distilled student model also outperformed post-training a single pre-trained model
(Supplementary Fig. 9d,e). Expression conditioning also preserved variant effect prediction: conditioning the
full-genome AG2 model on scRNA-seq datasets excluded from reference-condition pre-training and distillation38,53,
we found that expression conditioning matched reference conditioning on sc-eQTL sign prediction (Supplementary
Fig. 10a,c) and causality prediction (Supplementary Fig. 10b,d), on both the TenK10K and IBDverse datasets.
Additionally, expression-conditioning maintained performance on TenK10K sc-eQTL tasks even when taking
subsamples of 128, 16, or even a single cell per cell type (Supplementary Fig. 10e,f).

We then applied expression conditioning with the full-genome AG2 model to two studies unseen by AG2
(Fig. 3d). The first, the SARS-CoV-2 vaccination study above, identified vaccine-induced group A CD8+ T (VI-A
CD8+ T) cells, a rare population of SARS-CoV-2-specific CD8+ T cells that expands after booster vaccination48.
Chromatin accessibility was profiled in only 111 of these cells, separately from scRNA-seq, providing a sparse
readout of this state. Conditioned on the mean cell embeddings of each T-cell state from scRNA-seq alone, AG2
imputed accessibility differences between VI-A CD8+ T cells and transcriptionally similar effector memory CD8+ T
(CD8 TEM) cells that correlated with the observed differences (Pearson 𝑟= 0.46 across 37,035 predicted accessible
peaks; Fig. 3e; Methods), indicating that expression conditioning can recover chromatin differences in rare cell
states that are difficult to profile directly.
Zhang et al. reported that regulatory elements with increased accessibility in VI-A CD8+ T cells harbor variants
associated with COVID-19 severity, including near FYCO1 at the 3p21.31 locus, but did not link these variants to
target genes48. One such variant, rs13066062 (chr3:45976852:G>A), lies in a FYCO1 intron within a peak that
was more accessible in VI-A CD8+ T cells than in CD8 TEM cells in both observed and imputed data (Fig. 3e,f). ISM
in VI-A CD8+ T cells indicated that the variant disrupts a T-box motif compatible with TBX21 and EOMES, which
are expressed at higher levels in CD8 TEM and VI-A CD8+ T cells than in naive CD8+ T cells (Fig. 3f). Consistent
with the expression of these factors, conditioning AG2 on 881 metacells spanning all post-vaccination PBMCs
predicted loss of accessibility concentrated in CD8 TEM, natural killer (NK), and VI-A CD8+ T cells, with little effect
in naive T, B, and myeloid cells (Fig. 3g; Methods). AG2 also imputed higher H3K27ac at this peak in CD8 TEM
and VI-A CD8+ T cells than in naive CD8+ T cells and predicted that rs13066062 modestly reduces H3K27ac in
these states (Supplementary Fig. 10k). Across nearby genes, the variant was predicted to decrease expression
of the chemokine receptor gene CXCR6 more strongly than FYCO1 (Supplementary Fig. 10i); this effect was
concentrated in effector T-cell and NK metacells, with the two VI-A CD8+ T metacells ranking first and tenth of 881
(Fig. 3h, Supplementary Fig. 10j). These predictions nominate CXCR6 as a putative target of rs13066062, aligning
with 3p21.31 colocalization and eQTL analyses linking COVID-19 severity risk to reduced CXCR6 expression in T
cells54.
The second unseen study profiled the substantia nigra and cingulate cortex of 190 donors with and without
Parkinson’s disease by single-nucleus multiome sequencing (paired RNA-seq and ATAC-seq), mapped eQTLs
and chromatin accessibility QTLs (caQTLs) alongside allele-specific chromatin accessibility (ASCA), and trained
CREsted and fine-tuned Scooby models on these data49. Conditioned on the mean single-nucleus expression
embeddings of each cell type and brain region, AG2 predicted the effect direction of variants significant in both
accessibility analyses (caQTL-ASCA) more accurately than CREsted (sign accuracy–recall AUC 0.892 vs. 0.850;
Fig. 3i), and at the sign accuracy Scooby achieves at its reporting threshold for eQTLs (87.3%), AG2 reached
more than double the recall (41% vs. 17%, Fig. 3j). On both tasks, expression conditioning also matched or slightly
exceeded conditioning on matched reference conditions (caQTL-ASCA AUC 0.892 vs. 0.885; eQTL AUC 0.834
vs. 0.807; Supplementary Fig. 10g,h). Thus, without any training on this cohort, post-trained AG2 outperformed
sequence models trained on the cohort’s own data, demonstrating that expression conditioning extends AG2 to
unseen datasets without fine-tuning.
Predicting enhancer–gene links at scale
Mapping distal enhancers to their target genes across human cell types remains challenging: CRISPR interference
(CRISPRi) screens are largely limited to immortalized cell lines11,55,62, peak–gene correlation methods63 require
matched chromatin accessibility and expression data and confound regulation within a cell type with co-variation
across cell states64. Supervised classifiers such as ENCODE-rE2G(-Extended) and scE2G require experimental
chromatin assays in each target cell type55,64. S2F models instead estimate enhancer importance from the
sensitivity of predicted gene expression to the input DNA sequence14,16, but prior models are restricted to fixed and
mostly bulk training tracks. Because AG2 is trained across hundreds of primary single-cell reference conditions
and supports expression conditioning on new scRNA-seq profiles, it can predict cell-type-specific E2G links across
primary cell types and unseen single-cell datasets without requiring chromatin assays in the target cell type. To
score candidate E2G links in a target condition, we took the gradient of the model’s predicted gene expression
with respect to the 1-Mb input sequence centered on each gene’s TSS and aggregated the unnormalized gradient
magnitudes across each candidate element (Fig. 4a; Methods).
Using the distilled AG2 model trained across the full genome, we first evaluated E2G linking on the held-
out ENCODE-rE2G CRISPRi benchmark spanning 4378 element–gene pairs across five cell lines outside the
ENCODE-rE2G training set55. Conditioned on the matching reference conditions, AG2 achieved an AUPRC of
0.78, outperforming AG1 (0.73), a four-model Borzoi ensemble (0.61) and ENCODE-rE2G (0.57; Fig. 4b; K562
benchmark in Supplementary Fig. 11f). Whereas sequence models performed comparably within 10 kb of the TSS
(AUPRC 0.91–0.95), AG2’s advantage grew with distance, reaching an AUPRC of 0.62 at 100–2500 kb versus
0.48 for AG1, 0.37 for ENCODE-rE2G and 0.28 for Borzoi (Fig. 4b). On a CRISPRi screen in cultured primary
human astrocytes56 AG2 achieved an AUPRC of 0.68, surpassing AG1 (0.64), Borzoi (0.49) and the screen’s

a target cell type, candidate elements in a 1-Mb sequence centered on a gene’s TSS are scored from the gradient of predicted expression with
respect to the input sequence. (b,c) E2G linking AUPRC across all element–gene pairs and by TSS distance on (b) the held-out ENCODE-rE2G
CRISPRi benchmark across five cell lines55 (𝑛= 4378 pairs, 190 positives; Methods) and (c) a human astrocyte CRISPRi screen56 (𝑛= 1902
pairs, 122 positives), comparing AG2, AG114, a Borzoi ensemble16, ENCODE-rE2G (b), EGrf (c) and AG2 trained without bulk astrocyte
tracks (c). (d) E2G scores normalized to the window maximum for AG2, AG1 and ENCODE-rE2G across 𝑛= 63 CRISPRi-tested elements
(𝑛= 6 positives) at the MYC locus in HCT116 cells. (e) Genome-wide E2G atlas pipeline across 640 primary single-cell reference conditions
(Methods). (f) Consensus distal E2G pairs by number of active conditions across the 640 reference conditions (dashed line, median). (g) TSS
distance distribution for condition-specific (≤5% of conditions) versus ubiquitous (≥95%) consensus E2G pairs. (h) Active E2G links across
the BCL11A locus in 13 primary neural and hematopoietic conditions (500-bp bins colored by gradient score; dashed line, TSS; Methods).
Lineage-colored shading and arrowheads mark the +62/55 kb erythroid enhancer cluster57,58 and B-cell (+200 kb58) and cortical (+479 kb59)
promoter capture Hi-C contacts. (i) Zoomed view of BCL11A showing AG2-predicted bone marrow (BM) erythroblast scATAC-seq and active
E2G links (top) alongside experimental ATAC-seq and enhancer-probe Capture-Pore-C in wild-type (middle) and sg1617-edited (+58 kb GATA1
site; bottom) HUDEP-2 erythroid cells60. Experimental arcs show TSS contacts per million genome-wide paired-end tags per baited enhancer
(mean of two replicates; Methods); orange shading marks the +62, +58 (core) and +55 kb elements. (j) ISM logos of predicted BM erythroblast
scATAC-seq across the therapeutic guide RNA (gRNA) target site61 for REF and the GATAA-disrupting ALT allele (chr2:60495266:G>A;
orange shading). (k) Base-resolution predicted scATAC-seq across the +58 kb core enhancer for REF and ALT in BM erythroblasts and blood
naive B cells (orange shading as in j).
random-forest predictor EGrf (0.58), again with the largest gain at 100–2500 kb (0.40 versus 0.18 for EGrf; Fig. 4c).
Across all three CRISPRi benchmarks, omitting the window-wide attribution normalization used in prior work14,16
consistently improved accuracy across sequence models (Supplementary Fig. 11a–c). This performance did not
depend on bulk astrocyte data: the imputation-split distilled model, in which all bulk astrocyte tracks were held
out so that the astrocyte reference condition was trained only on the screen’s scRNA-seq pseudobulk, reached
an AUPRC of 0.72 (Fig. 4c; Methods), and expression conditioning on K562 and astrocyte scRNA-seq profiles
matched or exceeded standard reference conditioning (Supplementary Fig. 11d,e). Single-cell expression alone
can therefore suffice to map E2G links in primary cells. Likewise, in the unseen Parkinson’s disease brain cohort49,

expression-conditioned links at caQTL-ASCA peaks showed high Jaccard similarity specifically with predictions
from the matching brain reference conditions (Supplementary Fig. 11g).
To illustrate AG2’s gain on long-range enhancers, we examined MYC in HCT116 colorectal carcinoma cells
from the held-out benchmark. In colorectal carcinoma, MYC is regulated across a gene desert by a lineage-specific
super-enhancer ∼500 kb upstream that transcribes CCAT1 and loops to the MYC promoter65. All six CRISPRi-
validated enhancers among the 63 tested elements at this locus lie 470–521 kb upstream within or adjacent to this
super-enhancer (Fig. 4d). AG2 ranked five of these six enhancers among the top seven elements overall (ranks 1,
2, 3, 5, 7 and 24), whereas AG1 (ranks 24–53) and ENCODE-rE2G (ranks 14–51) missed this upstream cluster
and prioritized non-functional elements downstream of MYC (Fig. 4d).
We next applied AG2 across 640 primary single-cell reference conditions to build an atlas of E2G links spanning
39,323 protein-coding and long non-coding RNA genes (Fig. 4e; Methods). Using thresholds on AG2-predicted
target gene expression, local chromatin accessibility, and expression gradient magnitude chosen on the K562 and
astrocyte CRISPRi screens to retain nearly all validated enhancers and target genes while maximizing linking 𝐹1
on the astrocyte screen (Supplementary Fig. 12; Methods), we called and summit-merged active 500-bp genome
tiles located more than 500 bp from the TSS. This yielded a median of 64,800 distal links per condition at a median
TSS distance of 35.7 kb (mean 81 kb), and a median of 4 distal enhancers per regulated gene (Supplementary
Fig. 13a–h). When reconciled across conditions into consensus pairs (Methods), distal links proved highly condition-
specific: the median number of active conditions per pair was 3, and 65.1% of links were detected in 5 or fewer
conditions (90.0% in ≤5% of conditions; Fig. 4f). Including promoter-proximal elements (≤500 bp from the TSS)
showed that ubiquitously active links (≥95% of conditions) were predominantly promoter-proximal (89.8%), whereas
condition-specific links (≤5% of conditions) were more distal (99.4% >500 bp; Fig. 4g). Consistently, ubiquitously
expressed genes almost always had active promoter-proximal elements and, compared with condition-restricted
genes, more frequently lacked linked distal enhancers (Supplementary Fig. 13i).
Querying this atlas for a target gene resolves its lineage-specific regulatory architecture, which can then be
dissected at base resolution with AG2. We illustrated this at BCL11A, a transcription factor whose expression across
erythroid, lymphoid, and neural lineages is governed by distinct distal enhancers57–59. Across 13 hematopoietic
and neural cell types, the atlas recovered lineage-specific sets of distal enhancers that recapitulate experimentally
mapped promoter contacts up to nearly 500 kb away, including a +200 kb contact mapped in naive and total B
cells, which among the conditions shown AG2 links most strongly in naive B cells, and a +479 kb prefrontal cortex
contact in cortical interneurons (Fig. 4h). In erythroid cells, the highest-scoring links converged on the +62/55 kb
intronic enhancer cluster (comprising the +62, +58 and +55 kb elements) that drives erythroid BCL11A expression
to silence fetal hemoglobin57. In HUDEP-2 erythroid cells, these elements are accessible and contact the BCL11A
promoter, and CRISPR–Cas9 editing of the +58 kb enhancer GATA1 site reduces both60 (Fig. 4i; Methods). Within
the +58 kb enhancer, clinical CRISPR–Cas9 gene therapy for sickle cell disease and 𝛽-thalassemia disrupts an
essential GATA1 motif (GATAA) to reactivate fetal hemoglobin while sparing non-erythroid BCL11A function57,61,66.
In silico mutagenesis across the therapeutic guide RNA target site recovered this GATAA motif as the primary
sequence determinant of local accessibility in bone marrow erythroblasts, and a single-nucleotide substitution in
the motif (chr2:60495266:G>A) removed its attribution (Fig. 4j) and reduced predicted accessibility across the
+58 kb enhancer in erythroblasts, whereas naive B cells showed little predicted accessibility there for either allele
(Fig. 4k).
Interpreting complex-trait variants across cell types
Genome-wide association studies have identified thousands of loci associated with complex traits and diseases,
yet interpreting a non-coding association requires answering three questions that are difficult to resolve at scale:
which variant among those in LD is causal, which gene it regulates, and in which cell type it acts69,70. Because
AG2 predicts cell-type-resolved gene expression across a 1-Mb sequence window, scoring candidate variants with
high confidence across primary cell types can address all three questions simultaneously. To retrospectively test
this capability at an experimentally dissected disease locus, we examined the 21q22 intergenic locus associated
with inflammatory bowel disease (IBD) and other immune-mediated disorders, where Stankey et al. combined
chromatin conformation capture, CRISPR–Cas9 perturbation, and MPRAs in primary human macrophages to
show that rs2836882 drives disease risk by upregulating the distal gene ETS2, located approximately 290 kb away,
rather than closer candidate genes67 (Fig. 5a). Scoring the seven variants in the fine-mapped IBD credible set
across primary immune cell types of the ileum, AG2 recovered all three components of this mechanism: it predicted
a substantial effect for only rs2836882, linked the variant selectively to ETS2 despite its 290-kb distance, and
confined the effect to monocytes and macrophages (Fig. 5b).
To apply this approach systematically, we used the distilled AG2 model trained across the full genome to score
approximately 7 million variants, comprising fine-mapped GWAS credible sets from the Open Targets Platform27,71
and common population variants (minor allele frequency > 5%) from gnomAD72, across 510 conditions spanning
tissue and cell-type combinations (Fig. 5c; Methods). To define a stringent threshold for calling expression-altering

variants alongside their target genes and cell types, we calibrated predictions against fine-mapped sc-eQTLs
from the TenK10K study7, evaluating both enrichment for fine-mapped eQTLs over tested common variants and
discrimination of on-target effects from off-target pairs of gene and cell type where the variant had no detectable
effect (Methods). Requiring both a minimum predicted effect size and predicted target gene expression in the given
condition yielded an approximately 280-fold enrichment for fine-mapped sc-eQTLs and an approximately 25-fold
enrichment for on-target over off-target effects (Fig. 5d,e). Variants called at this threshold were predominantly
cell-type- or lineage-specific (Supplementary Fig. 14a), overlapped ENCODE cCREs (Supplementary Fig. 14b), and
were two- to five-fold more likely than uncalled variants to occupy evolutionarily constrained bases (phyloP ≥2.27;
Fig. 5f), with more lineage-restricted calls acting at greater distances from the target gene TSS (Fig. 5g). The
chosen threshold is likely conservative, because unassociated background variants vastly outnumber fine-mapped
eQTLs, so even a small fraction of true regulatory variants missed due to finite sc-eQTL mapping power can deflate
the observed enrichment.
We next asked whether variant calls in each condition recover the cell types and states relevant to individual
complex traits. Pooling credible-set variants for well-powered traits and testing for enrichment relative to background
common variants across all 510 conditions (Methods) identified at least one significantly enriched condition (false
discovery rate ≤10−7; one-sided Fisher’s exact test; Benjamini–Hochberg correction) for 17 (of 28) traits (Fig. 5h).
The prioritized cell types recapitulated established trait biology, including implicating prostate luminal epithelial cells
in prostate cancer, cardiomyocytes and sinoatrial node pacemaker cells in atrial fibrillation, microglia in Alzheimer’s
disease, and diverse immune lineages across autoimmune diseases (Fig. 5h). Across these 17 traits, the fraction
of variants called by AG2 is higher for variants with higher posterior inclusion probability (PIP), confirming that the
model preferentially prioritizes putatively causal variants over linked bystanders (Fig. 5i). This relationship was
substantially stronger within each trait’s top-enriched condition than when aggregating calls across all conditions
(Fig. 5i), and held equally for credible sets that do not colocalize with known eQTLs (Fig. 5j), indicating that AG2
provides information beyond existing eQTL studies. Moreover, even when collapsed across conditions and genes
to match cell-type-agnostic complex-trait benchmarks such as TraitGym73 (Methods), AG2 outperformed AG114
and matched the conservation-based predictor GPN-Star74, while a simple combination of AG2 and GPN-Star
outperformed all individual methods (Supplementary Fig. 14c).
Beyond nominating causal variants, target genes, and cell types, AG2 predicts the direction of variant-induced
expression changes. Combining the predicted expression sign with the direction of the GWAS trait association
yields a directional prediction for how increased gene dosage affects the phenotype, which should be opposite
to the effect of coding loss-of-function (LoF) variants identified by rare-variant burden tests (Methods). Across
nominated trait–gene pairs with rare-variant burden evidence, the direction predicted by AG2 was concordant with
LoF burden evidence in approximately 75% of all pairs and in 85% of pairs where the nominated gene was not the
nearest gene to the variant (Fig. 5k).

To illustrate how AG2 links non-coding trait variants to distal target genes and developmental cell states,
we examined the height-associated variant rs2038740, which lies proximal to the testis-specific gene TCP11.
Rather than implicating TCP11, AG2 predicted that rs2038740 upregulates SCUBE3, located 67 kb away, in adult
chondrocytes, where the trait-increasing T allele introduces positive sequence attribution immediately adjacent to
an intact SOX transcription factor binding motif (Fig. 5l). Because biallelic LoF variants in SCUBE3 cause a human
skeletal dysplasia with short stature and Scube3-deficient mice exhibit impaired endochondral bone growth75,
increased SCUBE3 expression in chondrocytes provides a plausible mechanism for increased height. Conditioning
AG2 on single-cell expression profiles from embryonic human limb development68, where longitudinal bone
growth is established, localized the predicted effect of rs2038740 to condensed mesenchyme and differentiating
chondrocytes (Fig. 5m). Although SCUBE3 is broadly expressed across early limb-bud populations, the predicted
variant effect tracked the expression of SOX9, a master regulator of chondrogenesis76 (Supplementary Fig. 14d,
𝜌= 0.47). Additional examples of locus-level variant interpretation across complex diseases are provided in
Supplementary Note 1.
Discussion
We have shown that modulating sequence representations early and throughout the network with a target condition,
together with training across bulk and organism-wide single-cell atlases, allows AlphaGenome 2 to better resolve
regulatory differences across tissues and closely related primary cell types. AlphaGenome 2 improves condition-
specific variant effect prediction on gene expression and E2G linking, and enables cross-modality epigenomic
imputation, including in unseen cell states profiled only by scRNA-seq. Across hundreds of human cell types,
we applied these capabilities to construct precomputed atlases of distal E2G links and variant scores for nearly
7 million trait-associated and common variants, nominating putatively causal variants, target genes with direction of
effect, and affected cell states even at GWAS loci lacking known eQTL colocalizations8.
Despite these gains, important limitations remain. At high precision, AlphaGenome 2 recovers only a minority
of fine-mapped eQTLs and putatively causal GWAS variants. Even in relevant cell states, AlphaGenome 2 can
underestimate causal variant effects, particularly for variants outside accessible chromatin, at very distal elements,
or acting through post-transcriptional mechanisms. Low recall also limits gene expression prediction across
individuals77–79. Coverage and resolution across cell states remain incomplete: transient, stimulated, and disease
states are underrepresented in training and in the precomputed GWAS panel80–82. The model can still struggle
to distinguish fine-grained states such as neuronal subpopulations. Because condition embeddings are learned
from observational, steady-state profiles, they reflect correlational rather than causal relationships, and it remains
unclear whether AlphaGenome 2 can predict regulatory responses to gene knockout, overexpression, or dynamic
cell-state transitions.
These limitations present opportunities to improve next-generation models. Closer analysis of why S2F models
struggle outside accessible chromatin, and to what extent this gap reflects eQTL fine-mapping errors, could
reveal regulatory layers missed by standard chromatin and expression assays. Adding more modalities, such as
single-molecule footprinting assays including Fiber-seq83,84, could improve low variant recall by covering more
biological mechanisms. Cell-state specificity could be improved by expanding the training data, for example
to deeply sampled brain atlases85–87. Training across additional species could broaden application scope and
improve predictive accuracy, as could training on perturbation data, both in cis and in trans88. Therefore, given the
flexible architecture of AlphaGenome 2, improvements could be made by extending the training data to additional
modalities, cell types, species, or perturbation experiments.
Beyond expanding training data, AlphaGenome 2 provides a modular foundation for downstream extensions.
Because expression conditioning enters the frozen sequence model through a lightweight adapter, analogous
adapters could map spatial transcriptomics89, natural-language descriptions90,91, or experimental perturbation
profiles into the same condition space. Furthermore, attribution methods92 that resolve sequence motifs could
be linked to the expression of transcription factors to construct gene regulatory networks underlying scRNA-seq
atlases. Although AlphaGenome 2 requires a separate model prediction per condition and is therefore more
computationally intensive than fixed-track multitask models, fast attribution and mutagenesis approaches such
as Multi-ISM93 could scale variant interpretation94 across rare variants95 and thousands of cell states, including
transient and disease states. As single-cell and spatial profiling continue to expand across human development,
tissues, and clinical cohorts1,2, where sample sizes rarely suffice for direct QTL mapping, AlphaGenome 2 provides
a versatile computational approach for bridging single-cell atlases and human genetics.

Methods
Terminology
We use the following terms throughout the Methods.
AG2 and AG1: Abbreviations for AlphaGenome 2 (this study) and AlphaGenome 114, respectively.
Condition: The tissue, cell line, cell type, or cell state for which AG2 makes a prediction. A condition is supplied to
the model either as a reference condition or through expression conditioning.
Reference condition: A condition defined by a composite ontology identifier (Ontology organization) and repre-
sented by a learned entry in the model’s dictionary of reference condition embeddings.
Expression conditioning: Conditioning AG2 on cell embeddings obtained by passing single-cell RNA-seq (scRNA-
seq) expression profiles through the cell encoder, typically averaged over a group of cells.
Output head and channel: AG2 has 14 output heads in Stages 1–3, plus a denoised single-cell gene expression
head added in Stage 4 (Output heads). Each head predicts a fixed set of channels, defined by technical or target
attributes (such as assay chemistry, suspension type, sequencing endedness, strand or ChIP target) and shared
across all conditions.
Modality: A class of molecular readout (for example, chromatin accessibility, gene expression, histone modification,
CpG methylation or chromatin contact maps), each predicted by one or more output heads.
Imputation: Predicting a track for a condition–channel pair that was not observed during training.
Training data acquisition and processing
AG2 was trained on bulk functional genomics datasets from the AG1 corpus14 together with newly curated bulk DNA
methylation, scRNA-seq, and single-cell ATAC-seq (scATAC-seq) datasets from public repositories (Supplementary
Table 2). For datasets inherited from AG1, we summarize key processing steps and modifications below; full
upstream details are given in Avsec et al.14.
Ontology organization
Biological conditions across all experimental assays and species are formalized using a unified multi-ontology
indexing scheme. Each biological condition is uniquely represented by a composite 5-tuple of compact uniform
resource identifiers (CURIEs):
𝑡onto =
(︀
𝑡CLO, 𝑡UBERON, 𝑡CL, 𝑡EFO, 𝑡NTR
)︀
,
(1)
spanning five standard biological ontologies:
• 𝑡CLO: Cell Line Ontology (CLO) identifiers, capturing immortalized and transformed cell lines.
• 𝑡UBERON: Uber-anatomy ontology (UBERON) identifiers, capturing tissues, anatomical systems, and organ
structures.
• 𝑡CL: Cell Ontology (CL) identifiers, capturing primary cell types and defined differentiation stages.
• 𝑡EFO: Experimental Factor Ontology (EFO) identifiers, also capturing cell lines.
• 𝑡NTR: New Term Request (NTR) identifiers, accommodating cell states lacking established ontology entries.
Data derived from single-cell modalities, CpG methylation, and primary-cell bulk experiments typically use
a combined UBERON term and a CL term, whereas bulk tissue and cell-line conditions typically use a single
UBERON, CLO, or EFO identifier.
The composite ontology tuple 𝑡onto groups experimental datasets that profile the same reference condition under
a single shared representation, so a single reference condition may be associated with multiple observed modalities.
Within a modality, experiments sharing 𝑡onto are grouped according to the output channels of that modality’s head
(Output heads, Supplementary Tables 5 and 6): experiments differing in channel-defining technical or target
attributes map to separate output channels under the same 𝑡onto, whereas replicate experiments sharing both 𝑡onto
and output channel are averaged into a single target track following AG114 (with paired-end and single-end bulk
RNA-seq, ATAC-seq, DNase-seq, and ChIP-seq experiments assigned to separate channels rather than pooled).
Bulk transcriptomic and epigenomic data
RNA-seq
Bulk RNA-seq data were obtained from ENCODE96, GTEx34, and recount397; apart from a small
number of recount3 additions, the corpus matches AG114. Processed base-resolution coverage bigWig files were

downloaded from ENCODE and recount3 (including GTEx read coverage files, which were obtained via recount3).
These were normalized to a genome-wide sum of 108 (100 reads per million; normalized jointly across forward and
reverse files for stranded libraries), and replicate experiments sharing a reference condition and channel attributes
(data source, poly(A)+ vs. total RNA enrichment, and paired-end vs. single-end sequencing) were averaged into a
single track. Alongside base-resolution coverage, gene-level targets for the bulk gene expression head (Output
heads) were compiled across GENCODE annotations (v46 for human, vM23 for mouse) as transcripts per million
(TPM) for each condition and channel, ignoring strand, and supervised on protein-coding and long non-coding
RNA (lncRNA) genes. For ENCODE tracks, we used the TPM values from the ENCODE gene quantification files of
every experiment pooled into the track, averaged across files. We excluded 41 ENCODE experiments whose TPM
profiles correlated poorly (Pearson 𝑟< 0.1) with expression derived from their coverage tracks. For GTEx tracks,
we used the GTEx v8 gene TPM from the GTEx Portal, averaged across donors within each tissue. For other data
from recount397, we averaged the recount3 gene TPM values across the samples of each cell type.
ATAC-seq and DNase-seq
Bulk accessibility datasets were obtained from the AG1 corpus14 and processed
identically except that replicate aggregation within each reference condition separated paired-end and single-end
experiments into distinct channels. As in AG1 and ChromBPNet17, read 5’ ends were shifted by +4/−4 bp for
ATAC-seq (Tn5 transposase offset) and 0/+1 bp for DNase-seq (DNase I offset), and bigWig coverage tracks were
normalized to a genome-wide sum of 108.
Transcription factor and histone ChIP-seq
Histone modification and transcription factor (TF) ChIP-seq fold-
change-over-control tracks were obtained from ENCODE96 via the AG1 corpus14, aggregating replicates per
reference condition separately for paired-end and single-end experiments.
CAGE
CAGE data were obtained from the AG1 corpus with an identical processing pipeline. These data were
originally obtained from FANTOM598. Stranded bigWigs were jointly normalized to sum to 100 million.
Contact map data
Contact maps were derived from ENCODE Intact Hi-C and 4D Nucleome Micro-C experiments across human
and mouse (including the H1 embryonic stem cell and human foreskin fibroblast Micro-C maps used by Orca99;
77 maps total across 73 human and 4 mouse reference conditions), selecting a single contact map source per
(assembly, reference condition) pair. Each contact map was extracted from its multi-resolution cooler file100 at
1000 bp resolution (before training-time interpolation to 2048 bp; see below) and balanced by Iterative Correction
and Eigenvector decomposition (ICE)101 using cooler. In contrast to AG1 and Orca, we did not apply adaptive
coarse-graining, as we found it led to overfitting. Contact map prediction accuracy is therefore not quantitatively
comparable to these two models.
Preprocessing
For each anchor bin 𝑖, we stored the balanced contact frequencies with downstream bins 𝑗= 𝑖+𝑑
for 𝑑∈{0, . . . , 𝐷−1}, where 𝐷= ⌈220/1000⌉= 1049 diagonal bins cover all pairwise distances within the
1-Mb receptive field at 1000-bp resolution. We then applied observed-over-expected distance normalization: for
each contact map, a genome-wide mean profile 𝑚𝑑was computed by averaging strictly positive (>0), non-NaN
balanced contact frequencies at separation 𝑑across all chromosomes, and each contact value was normalized
as 𝑦𝑖,𝑗= 𝑥𝑖,𝑗/𝑚|𝑖−𝑗|, so that 𝑦= 1 represents the genome-wide expectation at that separation, 𝑦> 1 indicates
enriched interactions (such as enhancer–promoter loops), and 𝑦< 1 indicates depleted or insulated contacts.
The normalized data were serialized as per-bin records indexed by global genomic coordinates, with metadata
recording 𝑚𝑑and per-chromosome offsets for each (assembly, reference condition) pair.
Training-time interpolation
At training time, the stored upper-triangular contacts were reconstructed into a
symmetric matrix and interpolated from 1000 bp to the 2048-bp pairwise resolution by area-weighted mean pooling,
weighting overlapping source bins by their fractional area of overlap. Unmappable (NaN) bins were excluded from
the weighted mean, and target bins with >25% NaN area overlap were masked as NaN.
RNA splicing
AG2 predicts the same types of splicing targets as AG1, comprising splice junctions (which model RNA-seq read
coverage across junctions), splice site usage (SSU, which quantifies the proportional usage of a splice site), and
splice site classification labels.
These targets were derived from the same ENCODE and GTEx RNA-seq datasets used for bulk RNA-seq, and
were grouped by ontology CURIEs consistent with the bulk RNA-seq processing pipeline. Unlike the bulk RNA-seq
16
.

coverage pipeline, the splicing pipeline used only paired-end libraries. GTEx coverage tracks are unstranded,
so we realigned the GTEx reads with STAR and inferred junction strand from splice-site dinucleotide motifs
(--outSAMstrandField intronMotif). This yielded three library types (ENCODE total RNA-seq, ENCODE
poly(A)+ RNA-seq, and GTEx poly(A)+ RNA-seq), each on the forward and reverse strand (Supplementary Table 6).
Read alignment
Paired-end RNA-seq reads (converted from name-sorted BAM files to FASTQ where nec-
essary) were aligned to GRCh38.p13 (GENCODE v32) for human and GRCm38.p6 (GENCODE vM23) for
mouse using STAR v2.7.11b102. Key STAR parameters included: a minimum splice junction overhang of 8 bp
(--alignSJoverhangMin 8), up to 20 multi-mapped alignments (--outFilterMultimapNmax 20), at most
4% mismatches per read length (--outFilterMismatchNoverReadLmax 0.04), and strand inference from
splice dinucleotide motifs (--outSAMstrandField intronMotif). The splice junction table (SJ.out.tab)
produced by STAR served as the raw input for all subsequent splicing targets.
Splice site usage
For each splice junction identified by STAR, the SSU ratio was computed at both the donor
and acceptor positions. Following the approach of SpliSER103 and AG1, a pileup-based extractor queried the BAM
file at each splice site position and counted: 𝑘, the number of reads spliced at that site (identified via CIGAR 𝑁
operations whose boundaries matched the site coordinate), and 𝑛, which included reads that spanned the site
without splicing and reads that skipped the site via an intron from a competing junction. Reads flagged as PCR
duplicates, those with mapping quality below 30, or base quality below 20 were excluded. SSU was computed as
SSU = 𝑘/(𝑘+ 𝑛).
Per-condition aggregation
Junction counts and SSU counts from all samples belonging to the same ontology
CURIE were combined into a single table. STAR junction coordinates were adjusted to 1-based exon-boundary
conventions. Junctions on mitochondrial chromosomes, those with unknown splice motifs, and unstranded junctions
were discarded.
Filtering and normalization
Per-condition junction counts were normalized to a common scale of 108 mapped
bases. For both GTEx and ENCODE, the normalization denominator estimated the total mapped bases (approxi-
mately total reads × read length). The two sources differed only in how this quantity was obtained: from bigWig
per-base coverage sums for GTEx, and from STAR read counts multiplied by mapped read length for ENCODE.
Each junction was annotated against GENCODE (v46 for human, vM23 for mouse) to identify novel (unannotated)
junctions. A minimum average count threshold of 1 normalized read across replicates was applied.
Cross-species dataset creation
Per-condition filtered junctions were merged across all tissues within each
species into a unified splicing dataset. Junctions were ranked by total evidence (sum of normalized counts ×
number of replicates, summed across all conditions), and the top 600,000 junctions were retained. Similarly,
donor and acceptor splice site positions were ranked separately by evidence within each species, retaining the
top 300,000 donor sites and top 300,000 acceptor sites alongside all annotated GENCODE sites (v46 for human,
vM23 for mouse). When a genomic position appeared as both a donor and an acceptor on different strands, the
assignment with higher total evidence was retained, and junctions using the discordant assignment were removed.
The final dataset contains, for each species: the normalized junction count per condition, the SSU ratios (SSUdonor,
SSUacceptor), and per-junction GENCODE annotation status.
Splice site classification labels
Splice site classification labels were computed from the SSU ratios at training
time, and were defined per condition rather than per assay. Each splice site has a position, a strand and one
SSU ratio per track, where a track is a (condition, assay) pair. A condition can therefore have up to three tracks
(ENCODE total RNA-seq, ENCODE poly(A)+ RNA-seq and GTEx poly(A)+ RNA-seq). As in AG1, we define the
donor site as the last nucleotide before the intron and the acceptor site as the first nucleotide after it. For a sampled
condition, we took all tracks of that condition and labeled a position as a forward-strand donor, forward-strand
acceptor, reverse-strand donor or reverse-strand acceptor if the corresponding site had SSU > 0 (at least one read
spliced at the site) in at least one of these tracks. All other positions were labeled as non-splice sites. Conditions
without splicing tracks contributed no classification loss. In the imputation split (Imputation split), the classification
labels of a condition were dropped entirely if any of its splicing tracks were held out. In contrast, SSU and junction
targets remained specific to each assay and strand channel.
CpG methylation data
DNA methylation profiles were obtained from two complementary human methylome atlases spanning distinct
experimental technologies: bulk whole-genome bisulfite sequencing (WGBS)28 and single-nucleus methyl-3C
