tissues of each donor, we assigned each cell type to its donor-majority site in the IBDverse single-cell atlas: for
each cell type, we retained donors with at least 20 cells of that cell type pooled across tissues, computed the
fraction of each donor’s cells originating from each tissue, and selected the tissue with the highest mean fraction
across retained donors. As peripheral blood cell types were already covered by the larger, better-powered TenK10K
cohort, we restricted this benchmark to the ileum and rectum and excluded cell types whose donor-majority site
was blood. Cell-type mappings aligned IBDverse labels to reference conditions in the donor-majority tissue, or to
the closest cell type from a related tissue where no match was available (Supplementary Table 10).
We evaluated IBDverse across all chromosomes rather than restricting to held-out test chromosomes (Variant
benchmarks split) because its smaller donor cohort yielded fewer high-confidence fine-mapped variants than GTEx
or TenK10K. To prevent overlap with variants used during model and scorer selection from other studies, we first
excluded all variants (both positive and negative) appearing on non-test chromosomes in the GTEx eQTL, GTEx
splicing QTL (sQTL), TenK10K sc-eQTL, and TenK10K single-cell chromatin accessibility QTL (sc-caQTL) causality
benchmarks. We then removed cell types with fewer than 10 high-confidence causal variants (PIP > 0.9). Together,
these filtering steps retained 3524 causal sc-eQTLs across 47 intestinal cell types: 34 with the terminal ileum as
donor-majority site (15 epithelial and 19 immune) and 13 epithelial cell types with the rectum as donor-majority site
(Supplementary Table 10). IBDverse was not used for model or scorer selection (Model selection).
Causality evaluation
We evaluated AG2’s ability to discriminate causal regulatory variant–gene associations
(PIP > 0.9) from non-causal pairs (PIP < 0.01) within each tissue or cell type.
Causal eQTLs are strongly enriched near TSSs and within active regulatory elements. To control for genomic
location, variants within each GTEx tissue, TenK10K cell type and IBDverse cell type were partitioned by overlap
with active regulatory elements, defined using ENCODE v4 SCREEN candidate cis-regulatory elements (cCREs)40
for GTEx, TenK10K MACS3 scATAC-seq peaks37 for TenK10K, and the union of TenK10K peaks and ENCODE
human colon46,126 MACS3 scATAC-seq peaks for IBDverse. For IBDverse, because our scATAC-seq training
corpus contained colon profiles but no terminal ileum or rectum datasets, we used 21 ENCODE colon tracks
(10x Chromium and sciATAC-seq) as the intestinal chromatin accessibility reference, calling MACS3 peaks per
experiment and merging them into 501-bp summit-centered intervals with SnapATAC2106 before taking the union
with TenK10K blood peaks. Each causal SNV was matched without replacement to the non-causal control in the
same stratum with the closest distance to its own target eGene TSS at a 1:1 ratio (GTEx: 𝑛= 67,674 total across
33,837 positives and 33,837 controls; TenK10K: 𝑛= 12,692 across 6346 positives and 6346 controls, IBDverse:
𝑛= 7048 across 3524 positives and 3524 controls). Here we defined the eGene TSS as the median TSS across all
transcripts of that gene in the GENCODE V46 GTF, as done in AlphaGenome114.
We computed a combined precision–recall curve by pooling across tissues in GTEx and cell types in TenK10K
or IBDverse. We additionally report tissue/cell-type-weighted AUPRC as done in AG1.
Effect direction prediction
For high-confidence causal variants (PIP > 0.9), we assessed classification accuracy
in predicting whether a variant increases or decreases expression of its target eGene (GTEx: 𝑛= 33,829, excluding
eight associations lacking allelic fold-change estimates; TenK10K: 𝑛= 6346, IBDverse: 𝑛= 3524). We evaluated
sign prediction performance across confidence thresholds by pooling instances across tissues or cell types,
ranking variants according to their predicted effect magnitude, and computing sign accuracy as a function of
recall (the fraction of top-ranked variants retained), summarized by the area under the sign accuracy–recall curve
(AUC). Additionally, we computed the tissue/cell-type-weighted sign-prediction area under the receiver operating
characteristic curve (AUROC) as done in AG1.
Effect magnitude correlation
For high-confidence causal variants (PIP > 0.9), we evaluated whether predicted
effect scores correlate with observed effect sizes, measured as allelic fold change in GTEx and single-cell regression
slope coefficients in TenK10K and IBDverse (GTEx: 𝑛= 33,829, TenK10K: 𝑛= 6346, IBDverse: 𝑛= 3524). We
computed the tissue/cell-type-weighted Spearman 𝜌as in AG1.
Scooby baseline
For TenK10K, we compared against Scooby21, using the published model trained on OneK1K
PBMC scRNA-seq. Scooby predicts single-cell RNA-seq coverage from a 524,288-bp input conditioned on a cell
embedding. We obtained a pseudobulk prediction for each cell type by summing predictions over all OneK1K cells
with that annotation, averaging forward and reverse-complement predictions. Each TenK10K cell type was mapped
to the closest of the 21 OneK1K annotations (Supplementary Table 9); eight had only approximate matches (naive
and intermediate B cells to B cells; innate lymphoid cells and proliferating natural killer (NK) cells to NK cells; AXL+
dendritic cells (ASDCs) and type 1 conventional dendritic cells (cDC1s) to dendritic cells; proliferating CD4+ and
CD8+ T cells to the corresponding effector memory cells). Inputs were centered on the variant, and variant effects
were scored as the log fold change, with a pseudocount of 1, of predicted counts summed over the eGene’s exons.

Because Scooby predicts only the central 196,608 bp of its input, eGenes with no exon in this window received a
score of zero; this affected 3.4% of causality and 3.6% of sign/coefficient pairs, all with the variant more than 35 kb
from the TSS, so the 0–35 kb strata (Supplementary Fig. 6a) compare all models on fully scored pairs.
Tissue-specificity stratification
To stratify tissue-specific versus nonspecific regulatory effects (Fig. 2d), GTEx
tissues were grouped by hierarchical average-linkage clustering on log-transformed median gene expression
profiles (cosine distance cutoff 0.1). A causal variant–gene pair (PIP > 0.9) was labeled tissue-specific (𝑛= 7169)
if it showed PIP > 0.01 in only a single tissue cluster and nonspecific (𝑛= 26,660) otherwise.
Single-cell eQTLs with expression conditioning
In addition, we evaluated the post-trained model (Stage 4:
Post-training for expression conditioning) on both the sign prediction and causality benchmarks for the Tenk10k
and IBDverse sc-QTLs. For Tenk10k, we supplied cell embeddings derived from an independent PBMC scRNA-
seq dataset unseen during pre-training (Gong et al.53) in place of learned reference condition embeddings
(Supplementary Fig. 10a,b). We subsampled this dataset to 1,000,000 cells, passed each cell through the cell
encoder, and averaged the resulting cell embeddings across all cells assigned to the same cell type to form the
mean cell embedding. Because this secondary PBMC dataset only contained cell-type matches for 25 of the 28
TenK10K cell types, the evaluation was restricted to this 25-cell-type subset. To assess sensitivity to the number of
cells averaged into the mean cell embedding, we also repeated both evaluations using random subsamples of 1, 16,
and 128 cells per cell type (10 independent trials per sample size). For IBDverse, we supplied the scRNA-seq data
derived from the IBDverse study38, averaged embeddings across cells from each cell type’s donor-majority intestinal
tissue, and evaluated the same 47 cell types described above (Single-cell eQTLs, Supplementary Fig. 10c,d).
Single-cell chromatin accessibility QTL (caQTL) evaluations
We used SuSiE fine-mapped cis-caQTLs from the TenK10K study37 (Supplementary Fig. 5b–e), where the target
regulatory feature is an ATAC-seq peak rather than a gene. As with single-cell eQTLs, AG2 was not trained on
TenK10K data; PBMC single-nucleus ATAC-seq profiles in our reference training corpus came from Benaglio et
al.110. For evaluation, models were conditioned on the target cell-type ontology term, and variants were scored
using the single-cell chromatin accessibility center-mask scorer on the channel corresponding to the reference
dataset. Cell-type mappings represent a best-effort alignment between TenK10K labels and the reference condition
set (Supplementary Table 9); as with sc-eQTLs, HSPCs were conditioned on the equivalent bone marrow cell type
in the reference corpus. Cell types with no fine-mapped positive variants (PIP > 0.9) residing in their target peak in
either the validation or test split were excluded from evaluation, leaving 24 evaluated PBMC cell types.
Two evaluation tasks were defined on held-out test chromosomes:
1. Causality classification: Causal SNVs (PIP > 0.9) residing within their target peak (𝑛= 5116) were
contrasted against non-causal in-peak SNVs (allele frequency >0.1, nominal 𝑃> 0.1, and absent from
SuSiE credible sets) drawn from peaks in the same cell type that contained at least one nominally associated
variant (𝑃< 0.01). Within each cell type, controls were matched 4:1 without replacement on distance to their
respective peak midpoint (𝑛= 25,580 variant-cell-type pairs total).
2. Effect-size correlation: Cell-type-weighted Spearman rank correlation (𝜌) between predicted scores and
observed caQTL slopes across all causal SNVs (PIP > 0.9, SNP inside its target peak; 𝑛= 5,116).
Variant effect benchmarks shared with AG1
For the remaining bulk variant effect benchmarks (GTEx splicing QTLs (sQTLs), rare splicing outliers, chromatin
accessibility and DNase sensitivity QTLs, and GTEx polyadenylation QTLs; Supplementary Fig. 2), datasets,
variant scorers, and metrics followed AG114, evaluated on held-out test chromosomes (Variant benchmarks split)
with AG2 conditioned on the corresponding tissue or cell-type reference condition. For polyadenylation QTL
(paQTL) causality on genes with multiple polyadenylation sites (PASs; 400-bp PAS mask), positive fine-mapped
variants within cumulative distance thresholds of 50 bp to 10,000 bp from the target PAS were matched 1:1 to
distance-matched negative controls across 𝑛= 100 random negative-sampling permutations to obtain per-threshold
AUPRC distributions (Supplementary Fig. 2e).
Locus-level variant vignettes
Locus-level variant analyses in Fig. 2f–h used the all-folds distilled AG2 model:

Single-cell expression autoencoder and post-trained model evaluations
Comparison of the post-trained model versus fine-tuning
To evaluate the post-trained model’s ability to reconstruct cell-type specificity of gene expression on held-out
genomic intervals (Fig. 3b,c, Supplementary Fig. 9d,e), we compared expression conditioning (Stage 4: Post-
training for expression conditioning) against a baseline model fine-tuned directly on each dataset. We evaluated
both approaches on a benchmark of single-cell RNA-seq datasets from CELLxGENE that were not part of the AG2
pre-training corpus, spanning datasets both seen and unseen during cell encoder training and AG2 post-training.
Benchmark construction
Starting from all human CELLxGENE datasets consisting entirely of primary data, we
retained those containing data with an assay and suspension type that matched a combination present in the AG2
single-cell gene expression channels. Within each dataset, we selected the dominant (tissue, assay, suspension
type) combination and did a best-effort manual grouping of its cell types into five broad lineages: immune, neural,
epithelial, endothelial, and stromal. Any lineage containing at least five cell types with at least 50 cells each formed
an evaluation group; if a lineage had more than 10 cell types, we kept only the 10 most abundant. Each evaluation
group therefore compares 5–10 related cell types that share the same dataset, tissue, assay, suspension type, and
lineage. Because datasets within a CELLxGENE collection often come from the same study and samples, we kept
only one evaluation group per lineage and collection: the one with the most cell types, with ties broken by the total
number of cells. A single dataset can still contribute evaluation groups for several lineages. This procedure yielded
167 evaluation groups in total: 147 (from 120 datasets in 118 collections) in the cell encoder training split and 20
(from 16 datasets in 16 collections) in a held-out split of datasets published after 1 October 2025 (Supplementary
Table 13). We note that the biased distribution of tissues in CELLxGENE is reflected in this evaluation.
Post-trained model
For each group of cells within a given cell type, tissue, assay, and suspension type, we
passed all constituent cells (or, for datasets exceeding one million cells, a cell-type-stratified subsample of up to
one million cells) through the cell encoder and averaged the resulting cell embeddings. We then conditioned the
fold-1 post-trained model on these mean cell embeddings to generate predictions.
Fine-tuning baseline
For each of the 167 evaluation groups, we fine-tuned the fold-1 distilled AG2 model
independently on the single-cell gene expression data of its source dataset. The reference condition embedding
table was re-initialized from scratch with entries for only the cell types in that group. The reference condition
embedding table and the FiLM layers were unfrozen while all remaining parameters were held fixed. This
configuration outperformed unfreezing only the reference condition embeddings on the LFC prediction task across
the eight CELLxGENE validation datasets (Single-cell expression autoencoder). Training used a batch size of
64 for 5000 steps at a constant learning rate of 10−4 without warmup, with transformer and sequence-to-pair
dropout disabled (retaining 0.1 weight dropout in the single-cell gene expression head) and only the single-cell
gene expression head active, restricted to fold-1 training intervals.
Base model comparison
We additionally post-trained a single Stage 1 pre-trained fold-1 model (prior to Stage 2
condition refinement; labeled “AG2 Post-trained from teacher”) using the identical Stage 4 procedure and compared
it against post-training the distilled student (Supplementary Fig. 9d,e).

Evaluation
Both approaches were scored using the cross-gene LFC Pearson correlation (Gene expression and
chromatin accessibility log fold change), computed across the cell types within each evaluation group. Metrics are
reported on held-out fold-1 validation intervals, which were not used for fine-tuning or model selection in these
comparisons.
Parkinson’s disease cohort sc-eQTL and caQTL evaluations
To evaluate variant effect prediction in an unseen disease cohort, we benchmarked AG2 on caQTLs and eQTLs
from the substantia nigra and cingulate cortex in a Parkinson’s disease single-nucleus multiome study (Sigalova
et al.49). Variants were evaluated natively on the Telomere-to-Telomere (T2T-CHM13v2.0) assembly and gene
annotation without liftover to GRCh38.
Conditioning
Gene identifiers in the cohort’s 10x Chromium snRNA-seq count matrices were mapped to the cell
encoder’s GENCODE v46 Ensembl vocabulary (protein-coding and lncRNA genes), and cell embeddings from the
cell encoder (unmasked all-folds model) were averaged within each (brain region, cell type) pair to form the mean
cell embedding (Stage 4: Post-training for expression conditioning).
Baselines
We compared against: (i) the published sequence-model predictions of Sigalova et al.49 (CREsted for
caQTLs and Scooby for eQTLs), and (ii) the all-folds distilled AG2 model conditioned on the closest brain reference
condition in our training corpus (Supplementary Table 14: dorsolateral prefrontal cortex, UBERON:0009834, across
23 cingulate cortex cell types mapping to 21 distinct reference conditions; midbrain, UBERON:0001891, for seven
substantia nigra cell types; and primary motor cortex, UBERON:0001384, for substantia nigra astrocytes).
Chromatin accessibility QTLs (caQTL-ASCA)
We evaluated the high-confidence caQTL-ASCA callset of
Sigalova et al., comprising variants with concordant effects (false discovery rate, FDR < 0.1) in both across-
individual cis-caQTL mapping and within-individual allele-specific chromatin accessibility (ASCA). Sign prediction
was evaluated against the reported allelic imbalance direction (𝑛pos = 43,526, 𝑛neg = 43,070) using the 1201-bp
single-cell chromatin accessibility center-mask scorer (Variant scoring), averaged across channels.
Expression QTLs
We evaluated cis-eQTLs (FDR < 0.05 across substantia nigra and cingulate cortex cell types)
that overlapped the caQTL-ASCA callset in the same (variant, cell type, brain region) tuple, retaining the minimum
𝑃-value variant per (gene, cell type, brain region) triplet. Restricting to target eGenes within the 1-Mb scoring
window yielded 1620 eQTLs (of 1739 total; 𝑛pos = 846, 𝑛neg = 774). Variants were scored with the single-cell
gene expression scorer (Variant scoring), averaged across channels for reference conditioning or read from the
target-strand channel of the denoised single-cell gene expression head for expression conditioning.
Scooby comparison
Sigalova et al. scored each caQTL-ASCA variant against all genes whose 524-kb Scooby
input window (centered on the gene body) contained the variant, reporting only predictions with |LFC| > 0.05 (the
fine-tuned Scooby model weights were not publicly available). We harmonized cell-type labels between the Scooby
predictions and the eQTL set, collapsed duplicate entries that differed only in peak identifier and had identical LFC,
and matched predictions to eQTLs on (variant, gene, cell type, brain region), yielding 276 Scooby-scored eQTLs.
eQTLs without a reported Scooby prediction were treated as abstentions, so Scooby was represented by a single
operating point: its recall was the fraction of eQTLs with a reported prediction, and its sign accuracy was computed
over those eQTLs.
Evaluation
For both tasks, sign accuracy was computed by pooling all (variant, cell type, brain region) instances,
ranking by predicted effect magnitude, and evaluating cumulative sign accuracy as a function of recall. Published
Scooby predictions were filtered at |LFC| > 0.05; the fraction of eQTLs retained by this filter defined Scooby’s
recall, and AG2’s recall was read off its sign accuracy–recall curve at Scooby’s sign accuracy.
Imputing accessibility and predicting variant effects in vaccine-induced CD8+ T cells
To evaluate whether post-trained AG2 can impute unseen epigenetic modalities and resolve cell-state-specific
non-coding variant effects purely from single-cell transcriptomic profiles, we analyzed a held-out multimodal PBMC
SARS-CoV-2 mRNA vaccination atlas comprising paired CITE-seq (scRNA-seq and surface protein) and ASAP-seq
(scATAC-seq and surface protein) profiles48. All evaluations used post-vaccination PBMC samples pooled across
days 11 and 28 across annotated level-2 T-cell states, including vaccine-induced group A CD8+ T cells (VI-A CD8+
T), effector memory CD8+ T cells (CD8 TEM), and naive CD8+ T cells (CD8 Naive).

Conditioning
As in the Parkinson’s disease cohort, we mapped CITE-seq scRNA-seq gene symbols to the cell
encoder’s GENCODE v46 Ensembl vocabulary (protein-coding and lncRNA genes), passed all post-vaccination
cells through the cell encoder, and averaged the resulting cell embeddings within each level-2 T-cell state to form
its mean cell embedding (Stage 4: Post-training for expression conditioning).
Consensus peak calling and differential accessibility evaluation
Observed ASAP-seq fragment files were
processed with SnapATAC2106 to call per-population peaks across level-2 PBMC cell states and merge them
into 236,854 501-bp consensus peaks; observed counts were summed per T-cell state and normalized to CPM.
We conditioned the post-trained AG2 model on the mean cell embedding of each T-cell state and imputed base-
resolution scATAC-seq tracks across all genome-wide evaluation intervals using the 10x nuclear scATAC-seq
channel. Predicted accessibility for each peak was obtained by averaging the base-pair-resolution predictions over
the consensus peak region, and was then normalized to CPM across peaks. We restricted the evaluation to peaks
predicted to be accessible (CPM ≥5 in VI-A CD8+ T or CD8 TEM; 𝑛= 37,035) and computed the log fold change
between VI-A CD8+ T and CD8 TEM as LFC = log(CPMVI-A + 1) −log(CPMTEM + 1), separately for observed and
imputed accessibility. We report the Pearson correlation between observed and imputed LFCs across these peaks
(Fig. 3e).
Locus analysis at rs13066062
To dissect the COVID-19 severity-associated variant rs13066062 (chr3:
45976852:G>A), we imputed base-resolution scATAC-seq tracks and 128-bp-resolution histone ChIP-seq tracks
across a 1-Mb interval centered on the variant conditioned on the post-vaccination mean cell embeddings for CD8
Naive, CD8 TEM, and VI-A CD8+ T cells. Observed ASAP-seq CPM bigWig tracks and imputed scATAC-seq tracks
were extracted across a 10-kb window (chr3:45971852-45981852), smoothed with a 128-bp uniform moving
average, and min–max scaled to [0, 1] across the three CD8+ T-cell states independently for observed and imputed
tracks (Fig. 3f). Imputed H3K27ac tracks (averaged across H3K27ac channels) were extracted unsmoothed over
the same 10-kb window on a shared raw signal axis without cross-state normalization (Supplementary Fig. 10k).
Saturated ISM was performed across the 41-bp window surrounding rs13066062 (chr3:45976832-45976873)
conditioned on VI-A CD8+ T cells using the 1201-bp single-cell chromatin accessibility center-mask scorer (Variant
scoring). Cell-state-resolved variant scores (ALT A −REF G) across CD8 Naive, CD8 TEM, and VI-A CD8+ T cells
were computed with the denoised single-cell gene expression scorer for CXCR6 and FYCO1 (Supplementary
Fig. 10i) and the 2001-bp histone ChIP-seq center-mask scorer (averaged across channels per modification)
across H3K27ac, H3K4me1, H3K4me3, H3K36me3, H3K27me3, and H3K9me3 (Variant scoring; Supplementary
Fig. 10k).
Metacell-resolved variant effects at rs13066062
To resolve variant effects across all post-vaccination PBMCs,
we embedded day 11 and day 28 CITE-seq cells with the cell encoder after randomly subsampling each level-2 cell
type to at most 10,000 cells (affecting only CD14+ monocytes). Within each level-2 cell type, cells were partitioned
into 𝑘= max(1, round(𝑛/64)) metacells by 𝑘-means clustering of their cell embeddings (scikit-learn127), yielding
881 cell-type-pure metacells (including two VI-A CD8+ T metacells spanning 115 cells). Conditioning post-trained
AG2 on each metacell’s mean cell embedding, we scored rs13066062 over a 1-Mb interval centered on the variant
using the 1201-bp single-cell chromatin accessibility center-mask scorer (10x nuclear scATAC-seq channel) and
the denoised single-cell gene expression scorer for CXCR6 (Variant scoring; Fig. 3g,h, Supplementary Fig. 10j).
For visualization, a uniform manifold approximation and projection (UMAP; umap-learn50, min_dist = 0.5, other
parameters default) was fitted on the per-cell embeddings. Each cell was colored by its metacell’s predicted effect,
and each metacell was placed at the UMAP projection of its mean embedding. Metacells were ranked by predicted
effect on CXCR6 expression, with rank 1 the largest predicted decrease.
Single-cell autoencoder evaluation
We compared 256-dimensional cell embeddings from the cell encoder (unmasked all-folds model) against an scVI
model51 trained directly on the CITE-seq RNA component of the SARS-CoV-2 vaccination PBMC atlas of Zhang
et al.48 across all four timepoints (𝑛= 113,897 cells across six donors and 30 level-2 cell types; Supplementary
Fig. 9a,b), using level-2 cell-type annotations as biological labels and donor identity as the batch variable.
Representations
For AG2, each cell was passed directly through the cell encoder without dataset-specific
training or batch covariates. For scVI (scvi-tools112), we selected the top 2000 donor-batch-aware highly variable
genes directly from raw UMI counts in Scanpy114 (Seurat v3 flavor115) and trained a two-layer, 30-dimensional
model with a negative binomial likelihood and donor batch covariate under default hyperparameters. Both latent
spaces were projected into two dimensions with UMAP in Scanpy114 (Supplementary Fig. 9b).

Benchmarking
We evaluated biological conservation (𝑘-means normalized mutual information and adjusted
Rand index, cell-type local inverse Simpson’s index (cLISI), and cell-type silhouette width) and donor batch
mixing (batch-removal-adapted silhouette score128) using scib-metrics52 without cross-method min–max rescaling
(min_max_scale=False), reporting the unscaled biological conservation, batch correction, and overall (0.6 ×
biological + 0.4 × batch) scores (Supplementary Fig. 9a).
Cell encoder denoising across cell counts
To evaluate how many cells are required for the single-cell
expression autoencoder to recover cell-type-specific expression profiles, we evaluated its denoised gene expression
reconstructions across datasets from the held-out CELLxGENE temporal split (Comparison of the post-trained
model versus fine-tuning, 𝑛= 16 datasets, Supplementary Fig. 9c). For each cell type and each subsample size
𝑘∈{1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64}, we performed 20 independent random subsampling trials without replacement, averaging
the embedding produced by the cell encoder across the 𝑘sampled cells in each trial and then decoding into a
single denoised profile; we also evaluated averaging over all available cells (All). Across all cell counts, these
denoised profiles derived from averaged cell embeddings were evaluated against the full observed pseudobulk
expression profile of that cell type using the cross-gene LFC Pearson correlation across cell types (Comparison of
the post-trained model versus fine-tuning). For each held-out dataset, we averaged the correlation across the 20
subsampling trials at each 𝑘, reporting both individual dataset trajectories and the median across datasets.
Imputation evaluations
Imputation metrics
Following the ENCODE Imputation Challenge43, evaluation metrics were chosen for (1) scale and offset invariance,
(2) sensitivity to condition-specific peaks and expression differences, (3) stratification by assay channel and
sequencing endedness, and (4) complementary assessment of discrete peak detection and continuous profile
shape.
Scale-dependent regression metrics such as mean squared error (MSE) were omitted during final evaluation
because technical scale and background differences penalize well-shaped profiles and because narrow histone
marks (such as H3K4me3) exhibit higher native variance than broad marks (such as H3K9me3)43. Instead, we
report genome-wide Pearson 𝑟for continuous profile shape alongside consensus peak AUPRC and cross-condition
gene LFC Pearson 𝑟for condition specificity.
Channel stratification and sequencing endedness
Imputation metrics and baselines were stratified by AG2
output channel (Supplementary Table 6), where each channel is defined by experimental modality, data source,
strand, ChIP target (transcription factor or histone modification), sequencing endedness (single-end vs. paired-end),
and suspension type for single-cell data.
Consequently, for DNase-seq, ATAC-seq, and ChIP-seq, we treated single-end and paired-end data as
distinct channels. Conflating single-end and paired-end tracks posed a major issue in the ENCODE Imputation
Challenge43, where the distributional shift between single-end training data and paired-end test data required
post-hoc reprocessing and quantile normalization. Modeling endedness as a channel attribute avoids cross-protocol
distribution shifts and prevents technical artifacts from confounding evaluation.
Consensus peak definitions and peak AUPRC
For each chromatin channel (defined by assay, ChIP target
where applicable, and sequencing endedness), we constructed a unified set of non-overlapping consensus peaks
with SnapATAC2106 by merging ENCODE narrowPeak calls across all profiled conditions in that channel (spanning
training and held-out splits). For each of the 176 held-out chromatin test tracks (spanning 25 of the 30 bulk test
conditions, as the remaining five held out RNA-seq only; Supplementary Fig. 7a, Supplementary Fig. 8a), we scored
each consensus peak by the mean of the predicted track across the 32-bp genomic bins spanning that peak (Metric
computation) and computed the AUPRC for classifying peaks called in the held-out condition (positives) versus
consensus peaks called only in other conditions of that channel (negatives), as in ChIP-seq peak AUPRC. Within
each modality (ATAC-seq, DNase-seq, histone ChIP-seq, and TF ChIP-seq), per-track AUPRCs were aggregated
via a weighted mean using each track’s positive peak fraction (the proportion of the channel’s consensus peaks
active in that condition) as its weight, which we refer to as support-weighted peak AUPRC (Supplementary Fig. 7b).
Condition specificity and peak frequency stratification
To assess model accuracy across regulatory elements
with varying degrees of biological selectivity (Supplementary Fig. 8b–e), consensus peak regions were stratified
by empirical peak frequency, defined as the proportion of conditions in which a consensus peak is active across
all profiled conditions in the assay channel. Peaks were partitioned into eight frequency bins of width 12.5%,
ranging from rare, condition-specific elements (active in 0 < 𝑓≤12.5% of conditions) to ubiquitous, constitutively
41
.

open elements (active in >87.5%). Peak AUPRC was evaluated independently within each frequency bin using
support-weighted aggregation across tracks, accompanied by an empirical random baseline reflecting the bin’s
support-weighted positive peak prevalence. This stratification decouples performance on invariant regulatory
landmarks from the model’s ability to capture condition-specific chromatin signal.
Gene LFC Pearson 𝑟
For bulk RNA-seq, genome-wide track correlations are dominated by housekeeping genes
expressed across conditions. For each protein-coding gene (GENCODE v46) completely contained within the
central 196,608-bp target window of a fold-5 test interval, we summed 32-bp bin coverage across the full gene body,
divided by the 100-bp read-length factor and gene length in kilobases, and clamped negative baseline predictions
at zero. Merging plus- and minus-strand tracks per biosample as in Gene expression and chromatin accessibility
log fold change yielded five evaluated RNA-seq tracks from the eight held-out tracks (181 evaluated tracks in total
across all bulk modalities). Given that the imputation evaluation spans tracks from heterogeneous RNA-seq assays,
we applied per-track uniform-quantile normalization across genes to the log(𝑥+ 1)-transformed expression values
prior to per-gene centering across the held-out tracks, and then computed the mean per-track Pearson 𝑟as in
Gene expression and chromatin accessibility log fold change.
Continuous signal correlations
For each held-out chromatin track, continuous Pearson 𝑟was computed
between observed and predicted profiles across all evaluated 32-bp genomic bins and averaged across tracks
within each modality (Supplementary Fig. 8f).
Metric computation
Evaluation metrics were computed per track at 32 bp resolution across the 1749 human
fold-5 test intervals (Imputation split). For each 1-Mb input sequence, spatial predictions were first cropped to the
central 196,608-bp target window (discarding 425,984 bp from each flank, as in Interval evaluations). Predictions
and observed targets at 1 bp resolution were downsampled to 32 bp via sum-pooling (with binary peak and gene-
body masks max-pooled), while 128-bp ChIP-seq tracks were upsampled to 32 bp by bin repetition and dividing
by the number of repeats to preserve total signal. Continuous 32-bp predictions and targets were concatenated
across all central 196,608-bp windows to compute genome-wide Pearson 𝑟. For discrete elements (consensus
peaks and protein-coding gene bodies), only features completely contained within each central 196,608-bp window
were retained and concatenated across windows to compute peak AUPRC, frequency-stratified peak AUPRC, and
gene LFC Pearson 𝑟.
Imputation baselines
To benchmark AG2 on bulk track imputation, we compared it against a retrained external baseline, Avocado, and
two training-track baselines: the cross-condition channel mean and an oracle that averages the most correlated
training tracks.
Mean training track baseline
For each test track, the mean baseline predicts the average signal across all
training tracks available for that channel. This baseline captures assay-specific signal profiles and invariant genomic
landmarks, but cannot capture condition-specific variation; for RNA-seq, because held-out tracks span distinct
channels (such as total and poly(A)+ protocols), channel-specific training means yield non-zero LFC correlation
without capturing condition-specific regulation.
Closest training tracks baseline
To establish an empirical upper bound for nearest-neighbor transfer, we
evaluated a closest training track oracle. For each evaluated interval, we identified the training tracks of the
same channel that had the highest Pearson correlation with the ground-truth test track, and averaged their signal.
Because selecting the closest tracks uses the held-out target track itself, this method serves as an oracle upper
bound for nearest-neighbor transfer. We averaged the five closest training tracks of the same channel (or all
available tracks if fewer than five existed); for the single test channel lacking same-endedness training tracks
(paired-end H3K79me2), the mean and closest baselines used single-end tracks.
Avocado multi-resolution tensor factorization
We trained the Avocado tensor factorization model45 across
the six bulk modalities in the imputation split (ATAC-seq, DNase-seq, CAGE, RNA-seq, histone ChIP-seq, and
TF ChIP-seq). Avocado models epigenomic tracks by learning latent representations for cell types, assays, and
multi-scale genomic positions, and then passing concatenated embeddings through an MLP to predict continuous
signal. Training followed the official two-stage protocol:
• Hyperparameter sweep on pilot regions: We conducted a 100-run random hyperparameter search on the
ENCODE pilot regions (∼1% of the human genome) for 800 epochs, sweeping cell-type and assay latent
42
.
CC-BY-NC 4.0 International license
perpetuity. It is made available under a 
preprint (which was not certified by peer review) is the author/funder, who has granted bioRxiv a license to display the preprint in 
The copyright holder for this
this version posted November 17, 2026. 
; 
https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.11.11.765857
doi: 
bioRxiv preprint 


dimensions (16 to 256), multi-scale genomic factors at 32 bp (5 to 25), 320 bp (10 to 50), and 6.4 kb (15 to
75), and MLP architectures (depth 0 to 4, width 128 to 2048).
• Genome-wide fine-tuning and model selection: The top 10 candidate models ranked by validation
MSE were fine-tuned across all human autosomes (chromosomes 1–22) and sex chromosomes (X and Y).
Genomic position embeddings were fine-tuned separately per chromosome for an extended 800 epochs
(compared to the default 400 epochs). The final model was selected based on validation set performance
across our primary biological evaluation metrics (support-weighted peak AUPRC).
• Resolution and preprocessing: To maintain native compatibility with our evaluation pipeline and track
resolutions without lossy downsampling, we adapted Avocado from its original 25 bp resolution to 32 bp
resolution. Consequently, the multi-scale genomic factors originally defined at 25 bp, 250 bp, and 5 kb
were shifted to 32 bp, 320 bp, and 6.4 kb, respectively. High-resolution 1-bp tracks (ATAC-seq, DNase-seq,
CAGE, RNA-seq) were downsampled to 32 bp via sum-pooling, and 128-bp tracks (histone ChIP-seq and TF
ChIP-seq) were upsampled to 32 bp by bin repetition and dividing by the number of repeats. Input tracks
were normalized using an arcsinh transformation prior to training and inverted with sinh at inference.
Primary PBMC enhancer annotation
We evaluated primary-cell H3K27ac imputation using the all-folds distilled imputation-split model (Imputation split),
in which all bulk PBMC tracks were withheld from training so that the nine peripheral blood immune reference
conditions (UBERON:0000178 paired with CL:0001054 CD14+ monocyte, CL:0000236 B cell, CL:0000787
memory B cell, CL:0000788 naive B cell, CL:0000895 naive CD4+ T cell, CL:0000900 naive CD8+ T cell, CL:
0000907 central memory CD8+ T cell, CL:0000913 effector memory CD8+ T cell, and CL:0000623 natural killer
cell) were trained exclusively on scRNA-seq and scATAC-seq pseudobulks. Candidate elements were ENCODE
GRCh38 representative DNase I hypersensitive sites (rDHSs; ENCFF708KEK) fully contained within genome-wide
evaluation intervals. Following the ENCODE cCRE convention to capture flanking nucleosomes, each rDHS was
expanded by ±500 bp on each side when scoring histone ChIP-seq and scored unexpanded for scATAC-seq. Mean
signal per element was log(𝑥+ 1)-transformed and 𝑧-scored across all genome-wide rDHSs independently for
each cell type and assay.
To classify elements with enhancer-like signatures (Supplementary Fig. 7d), we restricted within each cell
type to accessible (𝑧scATAC ≥1.67), non-promoter rDHSs (midpoint ≥200 bp from the nearest GENCODE v46
protein-coding or lncRNA TSS) and labeled elements positive if held-out ENCODE primary PBMC Mint-ChIP-seq
H3K27ac fold-change-over-control signal46 satisfied 𝑧H3K27ac ≥1.67. Within each cell type, we computed AUPRC
(as average precision) comparing AG2-imputed H3K27ac (paired-end ENCODE histone ChIP-seq channel) against
observed primary scATAC-seq (10x nuclear channel) and observed H3K27ac ChIP-seq from lineage-matched
ENCODE cell lines (HL-60 for CD14+ monocytes; GM12878 for B, memory B, and naive B cells; and DND-41 for
CD4+ T, CD8+ T, and NK subsets). To evaluate cell-type specificity across all genome-wide rDHSs (Supplementary
Fig. 7e), each site’s mean 𝑧-score across the nine PBMC cell types was subtracted independently for imputed and
observed Mint-ChIP H3K27ac, and Pearson 𝑟was computed across all pooled (rDHS, cell type) pairs.
Enhancer–gene linking evaluations
Enhancer–gene scoring
We scored E2G pairs using input gradients of predicted gene expression, using the same procedure for the E2G
benchmarks reported here and for genome-wide inference. For each gene, we centered a 1-Mb input interval on its
median TSS and conditioned the model on the reference condition of interest. We then defined a scalar predicted
expression readout ˆ𝑦𝑔for the target gene 𝑔from one of two heads:
• Bulk RNA-seq: predicted base-resolution coverage averaged across observed channels on the gene’s
strand, summed over annotated exonic positions of 𝑔(GENCODE v46) to give ˆ𝑒𝑔, and transformed as
ˆ𝑦𝑔= log(ˆ𝑒𝑔+ 10−6).
• Single-cell gene expression: predicted scalar gene-level expression for 𝑔averaged across observed
channels on the gene’s strand (ˆ𝑒𝑔) and transformed as ˆ𝑦𝑔= log(ˆ𝑒𝑔+ 10−6).
For both heads, we used only channels where the reference condition was observed during training (i.e.,
imputed channels were omitted).
We computed the gradient of ˆ𝑦𝑔with respect to the one-hot input sequence and took, at each position, the
gradient for the reference base minus the mean gradient across the four bases. The score for an enhancer was
the mean of the absolute attribution over a 2400-bp window centered on the enhancer midpoint, weighted by a
Gaussian kernel (𝜎= 300 bp) centered on the midpoint. Enhancers whose midpoint fell outside the input interval
(more than 524,288 bp from the TSS) received a score of zero.

In contrast to previous work14,16, which normalized enhancer gradient scores by the mean absolute attribution
across the entire input window, we omitted this window-wide normalization for all evaluated models, as we found
that using unnormalized scores improved performance across all models (Supplementary Fig. 11a–c).
Expression-conditioned enhancer–gene scoring
To evaluate E2G linking without relying on predefined
reference conditions, we also scored the astrocyte56 and ENCODE-rE2G K56255 CRISPRi benchmarks using
expression conditioning (Stage 4: Post-training for expression conditioning). For each benchmark, we passed
the single-cell RNA-seq profiles from the corresponding dataset through the cell encoder (Single-cell expression
autoencoder) and averaged the resulting cell embeddings to form the mean cell embedding. Conditioning the
post-trained AG2 model on this embedding, we defined the scalar readout ˆ𝑦𝑔from the denoised single-cell gene
expression head on the target gene’s strand and computed Gaussian-weighted input-gradient attribution scores
identically to the reference-conditioned setting.
Enhancer–gene linking benchmarks
Held-out CRISPRi benchmark
We evaluated E2G linking on the held-out CRISPRi benchmark from ENCODE-
rE2G55, which comprises element–gene pairs tested in K562, WTC11, Jurkat, GM12878 and HCT116 cells that do
not overlap the ENCODE-rE2G training data. After updating outdated gene symbols to match GENCODE v46,
all 4378 pairs (190 positives) were retained. By distance from the element to the gene TSS, there were 58 pairs
(30 positives) within 10 kb, 670 (87) at 10–100 kb, 3600 (73) at 100–2500 kb and 50 (0) beyond 2.5 Mb. By cell
line: K562, 1918 (118); WTC11, 1921 (15); HCT116, 396 (34); Jurkat, 75 (7); GM12878, 68 (16). Following the
authors, each pair was weighted by the probability that it reflects a direct rather than indirect effect, as provided by
the benchmark55.
K562 CRISPRi benchmark
We also evaluated E2G linking on the K562 CRISPRi benchmark assembled in the
ENCODE-rE2G study55. Following the authors, we retained element–gene pairs with a defined regulatory label
whose target gene belonged to the ENCODE-rE2G gene universe (10,356 pairs, 471 positives), and excluded
three negative pairs whose target gene Ensembl identifiers were absent from GENCODE v46, yielding 10,353
element–gene pairs (471 positives). Using the element-to-TSS distances provided with the benchmark, there were
221 pairs (131 positives) within 10 kb of the TSS, 1597 pairs (257 positives) at 10–100 kb, and 8535 pairs (83
positives) at 100–2500 kb.
Astrocyte CRISPRi benchmark
We used the CRISPRi screen of candidate enhancers in cultured human
astrocytes from ref.56. Following the authors’ criteria, positives were defined as element–gene pairs with a
significant decrease in expression (FDR < 0.1 and a negative log fold change, termed “permissive hits” by the
authors) whose element overlaps an ENCODE candidate regulatory element. Negatives were all other tested pairs
whose element overlaps an ENCODE candidate regulatory element. This procedure yielded 1902 pairs, of which
122 were positives.
Scoring with AG2
All E2G benchmarks were scored using the all-folds distilled AG2 model. For each element–
gene pair, AG2 was conditioned on the cell line or cell type in which the pair was tested. For the held-out CRISPRi
benchmark, the model was conditioned on the corresponding cell line (K562, WTC11, Jurkat, GM12878 or HCT116),
and ˆ𝑦𝑔was computed from bulk RNA-seq predictions (between one track per gene for Jurkat and six for GM12878).
For the astrocyte benchmark, the model was conditioned on the brain astrocyte reference condition (CL:0000127,
UBERON:0000955), and ˆ𝑦𝑔was computed from single-cell gene expression predictions. The single-cell RNA-seq
training data for this condition was a pseudobulk of all 47,577 cells profiled in the screen itself56, so this output
corresponded to the cells in which the element–gene pairs were tested.
To test whether AG2 can link enhancers to genes without any observed bulk data for the cell type, we also
evaluated the model trained with the imputation split enabled (Imputation split; “AG2 (no bulk astrocytes)”). In this
split, all human astrocyte bulk tracks (RNA-seq, CAGE, splice junctions, DNase-seq, and histone and TF ChIP-seq)
were held out from training, while the brain astrocyte single-cell RNA-seq tracks from the screen were retained.
The screen pseudobulk was therefore the only training data for this condition. This model was conditioned and
scored identically to AG2 on the astrocyte benchmark.
Baselines
For AG114 and Borzoi16, which are not conditioned, we applied the same scoring procedure to their
bulk RNA-seq tracks whose ontology term matched the tested cell line or cell type, using the same track criteria
and averaging as for AG2. For the held-out CRISPRi benchmark, this selected the same assays as for AG2: one
to three tracks per gene for AG1, and one to 14 for Borzoi, which provides one track per ENCODE experiment.

Neither model has single-cell outputs, so for the astrocyte benchmark we used their ENCODE astrocyte RNA-seq
tracks (CL:0000127). For Borzoi, we averaged the predictions of the four released replicate models and computed
gradients of this ensemble prediction. Borzoi uses a 524,288-bp input window, so elements more than ∼262 kb
from the TSS lie outside its input and were assigned a score of zero.
We also compared against three predictors that do not use a sequence model. For the held-out CRISPRi
benchmark, we used the published ENCODE-rE2G predictions for the same pairs55. For the astrocyte benchmark,
we used the EGrf predictions released with the dataset56. For both benchmarks, we also scored each pair by the
inverse of its distance to the TSS. All methods were evaluated on the same set of pairs.
Metrics
We report AUPRC, computed as average precision. For the held-out CRISPRi benchmark, pairs were
weighted by their direct-effect probability as described above; astrocyte pairs were unweighted. We computed
AUPRC across all pairs and within TSS-distance bins (0–10 kb, 10–100 kb and 100–2500 kb), skipping bins without
positives.
Expression-conditioned enhancer–gene linking in the Parkinson’s disease cohort
In the cingulate cortex of the Parkinson’s disease single-nucleus multiome cohort49, we compared E2G links
predicted via expression conditioning against those predicted from matched brain reference conditions (Supple-
mentary Fig. 11g). Predictions were generated natively on the T2T-CHM13v2.0 assembly and gene annotation
without liftover to GRCh38 across protein-coding genes (𝑛= 19,748). We evaluated the 21 cingulate cortex cell
types with distinct dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (UBERON:0009834) reference condition proxies (Supplementary
Table 14; L5 IT A and Lamp5 Exc were excluded because they shared reference proxies with L5 IT B and Lamp5,
respectively, and immune and unclassified neuron clusters lacked reference proxies). Each cell type was scored
under (i) expression conditioning on the mean 10x snRNA-seq cell embedding using the denoised single-cell
gene expression head, and (ii) reference conditioning on its matched proxy using the standard single-cell gene
expression head.
We evaluated candidate E2G links between the 19,898 caQTL-ASCA chromatin accessibility peaks (FDR ≤0.1)
identified in the Parkinson’s disease study49 and protein-coding genes within the 1-Mb scoring window. Mapping
each peak to all 500-bp tiles on our genome-wide scoring grid overlapping it by at least 250 (Genome-wide
candidate tiling and scoring), we called a (peak, gene) pair active if it passed all three atlas thresholds on predicted
target gene expression, local chromatin accessibility, and input-gradient attribution (Threshold selection). To
quantify cell-type specificity and agreement across conditioning modes, we computed the pairwise Jaccard
similarity between the sets of called (peak, gene) links for each of the 21 expression-conditioned cell types and
each cell type’s reference condition proxy (Supplementary Fig. 11g, left), alongside the pairwise Jaccard similarity
among the reference condition proxies themselves across the same 21 × 21 cell-type layout as a baseline of
cross-cell-type similarity (Supplementary Fig. 11g, right).
Complex trait evaluations
TraitGym evaluation
To evaluate zero-shot prioritization of non-coding variants implicated in complex human traits, we benchmarked
AG2 on the TraitGym complex traits dataset73. This benchmark comprises 11,400 variants, contrasting fine-mapped
GWAS causal variants (PIP > 0.9) against negative control variants matched for distance to the nearest TSS,
minor allele frequency, and linkage disequilibrium (LD) score at a 1:9 positive-to-negative ratio.
AG2 variant scoring
We used precomputed variant scores across 500 prioritized primary single-cell reference
conditions (a subset of the 504 reference conditions in GWAS panel), retaining only experimentally observed
(non-imputed) tracks. For each of the eight profile modalities (1-bp bulk ATAC-seq, DNase-seq, CAGE, bulk
RNA-seq, CpG methylation, and scATAC-seq; 128-bp histone and TF ChIP-seq), each observed track was scored
as the ℓ2 norm of log(ˆ𝑦ALT + 1) −log(ˆ𝑦REF + 1) across the 1-Mb window centered on the variant. For single-cell
gene expression, each observed channel was scored as the ℓ2 norm of predicted gene-level variant scores ∆expr
across all annotated genes in the window. The final zero-shot score per variant was the maximum score across all
observed tracks and all nine modalities.
Baselines and ensemble
We compared AG2 against three baselines: (i) AG114, using its zero-shot maximum
score across all output tracks spanning its six corresponding modalities (bulk ATAC-seq, CAGE, histone modification
ChIP-seq, transcription factor ChIP-seq, DNase-seq, and bulk RNA-seq); (ii) CADD129 whole-genome PHRED-
scaled pathogenicity scores; and (iii) GPN-Star74 scores (negated so that higher values indicate greater predicted

We evaluated all methods across the unified variant set using AUPRC (computed as average precision).
Precomputed variant and enhancer–gene linking scores
GWAS panel
We used the all-folds distilled AG2 model to generate an atlas of scores for 6,825,839 variants across 510 conditions
(504 reference conditions and 6 conditions evaluated via Stage 4 expression conditioning).
Variant set
We scored the union of three sources of common and trait-associated SNVs. First, all gnomAD72
variants with a genome allele frequency strictly between 0.05 and 0.95 (6,127,336 variants). Second, all SNVs
in Open Targets Platform27 release 26.03 GWAS credible sets that were fine-mapped with SuSiE (Open Targets
confidence ≥3, i.e. SuSiE with in-sample or out-of-sample LD; loci fine-mapped with Probabilistic Identification of
Causal SNPs (PICS) only were excluded), giving 1,879,681 variants. Third, all variants of the TraitGym complex-
traits benchmark73 (11,400 variants; mitochondrial variants excluded). Indels were excluded, and every variant
was verified to have a reference allele that matches the GRCh38 (hg38) reference sequence before scoring; no
mismatches were found. The final deduplicated set comprises 6,825,839 unique SNVs on chromosomes 1–22 and
X, each scored across 510 conditions. Of these 3,709 SNVs are unique to TraitGym (3,675 matched controls, 34
positives). The remaining 6,822,130 SNVs were used for GWAS analysis.
Scored conditions
We scored 510 primary human conditions: 504 reference conditions selected to maximize
tissue and cell-type coverage across our single-cell corpus, plus six bone-marrow and endometrial cell states
scored via Stage 4 expression conditioning using mean cell embeddings from the cell encoder (osteoblasts,
preosteoblasts, osteoclasts, and megakaryocytes from CZ CELLxGENE Discover dataset 1a2e3350-28a8-
4f49-b33c-5b67ceb001f6, and decidualized and non-decidualized endometrial stromal cells from dataset
9ddea8d9-cc4c-420a-90f6-880996f808d4130). The conditions are listed in Supplementary Table 15.
E2G atlas
We used the all-folds distilled AG2 model to generate an atlas of E2G linking predictions across 640 primary
single-cell reference conditions, annotating all human protein-coding and lncRNA genes in GENCODE v46
(𝐺= 39,323).
Genome-wide candidate tiling and scoring
For each biological condition and target gene, a 1-Mb input
sequence window was centered on the median TSS, and contiguous 500-bp sliding-window tiles (with 250-bp
step size) were scored across the ±524,288 bp interval. Candidate tiles were evaluated using a paired backward
and forward pass over the 1-Mb reference sequence. First, following Enhancer–gene scoring, we computed the
input gradient of the scalar log-transformed predicted expression readout ˆ𝑦𝑔with respect to the one-hot reference
sequence, using the single-cell gene expression head for reference conditions or the denoised single-cell gene
expression head under expression conditioning. Each candidate tile was assigned its Gaussian-weighted mean
absolute gradient score, stored alongside the model’s scalar reference gene expression prediction for gene 𝑔.
Second, a complementary forward pass extracted predicted base-resolution scATAC-seq profiles across the same
1-Mb interval, scoring each tile by the maximum predicted base-resolution signal within the tile per channel,
averaged across channels.
Threshold selection
Candidate E2G links were filtered through a three-stage gating pipeline requiring thresholds
on (i) predicted target gene expression, (ii) predicted local chromatin accessibility, and (iii) input-gradient attribution,
calibrated against CRISPRi perturbation benchmarks in primary human astrocytes (UBERON:0000955_CL:
0000127) and K562 cells (EFO:0002067)55,56:
• Target gene expression gate (ˆ𝑦𝑔≥0.5): To ensure that links were called only for transcriptionally active
target genes in a given condition, we required a threshold on the scalar predicted log-transformed gene
expression ˆ𝑦𝑔(averaged across observed channels on the gene’s strand before log transformation; Enhancer–
gene scoring). A threshold of ˆ𝑦𝑔≥0.5 retained nearly all CRISPRi-validated target genes in both astrocytes
(97.7%) and K562 (96.7%), while excluding 69.7% and 68.3% of all GENCODE v46 genes in astrocytes and
K562, respectively, consistent with the expectation that only a subset of genes is expressed in any given cell
type.

• Local chromatin accessibility gate (scATAC ≥1.25): To restrict candidate tiles to accessible regions in the
target condition, we required a predicted local scATAC-seq score ≥1.25, retaining 100% of CRISPRi-validated
positive enhancers in astrocytes and 98.9% in K562 while excluding 98.4% and 93.4% of distal inaccessible
tiles (>2 kb from both the target gene TSS and any ENCODE ATAC-seq narrowPeak), respectively.
• Gradient attribution threshold (score ≥0.0012): To call active E2G links among accessible candidate tiles
of expressed genes, we required a threshold on the Gaussian-weighted input-gradient attribution score. A
threshold of 0.0012 maximized the 𝐹1 score in the astrocytes benchmark (𝐹1 = 0.65, precision = 0.69, recall
= 0.61), while being within the near-optimal plateau for K562 (𝐹1 = 0.74, precision = 0.71, recall = 0.77).
Summit-based tile merging and summary statistics
To define active E2G links and compare them across
biological conditions, we applied a three-step consolidation procedure:
1. Per-gene summit tile extraction: Candidate tiles passing all three thresholds were designated as active
tiles. Within each biological condition and target gene, we grouped strictly overlapping active tiles and only
retained the tiles with the maximum gradient attribution score within each group (summit tiles); the remaining
tiles were discarded.
2. Within-condition enhancer merging: Within each biological condition, overlapping 500-bp per-gene summit
tiles were merged across all regulated target genes into mutually disjoint enhancer intervals, and every target
gene whose summit tile fell within a merged interval was linked to that shared enhancer locus.
3. Cross-condition consensus reconciliation: To compute statistics across the 640 biological conditions, per-
condition enhancer intervals linked to the same target gene were reconciled into cross-condition consensus
enhancer intervals via greedy clustering at a ≥50% mutual reciprocal overlap threshold (overlap(𝐴, 𝐵) ≥
0.5 × max(|𝐴|, |𝐵|)). Intervals were processed in decreasing order of the number of conditions in which that
E2G link was observed. Each interval was either retained as a new cluster representative or assigned to the
previously retained interval with which it had the largest reciprocal overlap; the consensus interval of each
cluster was the coordinates of its representative interval rather than the union of its members.
Promoter-proximal and distal tiles were processed jointly in all three steps. Whereas the 1-Mb scoring window was
centered on each gene’s median TSS across transcripts, TSS distances and promoter-proximal (≤500 bp) versus
distal (>500 bp) labels were measured from the enhancer interval midpoint to the gene’s representative TSS in
GENCODE v46 (the start of its MANE Select transcript131, or its Ensembl canonical transcript if absent). Because
classification was performed per link, a shared enhancer interval can be promoter-proximal to one gene and distal
to another. Unless stated otherwise, reported summary statistics refer to distal E2G links.
Per-condition yields (Supplementary Fig. 13d–f; Supplementary Table 16) and pooled link distributions (Sup-
plementary Fig. 13a–c,g) were both computed directly on the within-condition merged enhancer intervals from
step 2 (prior to step 3 cross-condition reconciliation). For each condition, we report the number of predicted active
distal enhancer intervals (Supplementary Fig. 13d), distal E2G links (Supplementary Fig. 13e), and target genes
with at least one distal link (Supplementary Fig. 13f). Pooling step 2 intervals across all 640 conditions, we also
report distributions of (i) enhancer-to-TSS distance, measured from the enhancer interval midpoint to the linked
gene’s representative TSS (Supplementary Fig. 13a); (ii) distinct target genes linked per (condition, enhancer
interval) (Supplementary Fig. 13b); (iii) distinct distal enhancer intervals linked per regulated (condition, gene) pair
(Supplementary Fig. 13c); and (iv) within-condition merged enhancer interval length (Supplementary Fig. 13g).
The cross-condition consensus intervals from step 3 were used only for consensus enhancer interval lengths
(Supplementary Fig. 13h) and cross-condition specificity analyses, defined by the number of conditions in which
each consensus E2G link was active (Fig. 4f,g).
Locus analysis at BCL11A
For BCL11A (ENSG00000119866; MANE Select TSS at chr2:60553654), we
retrieved within-condition merged active E2G links (promoter-proximal and distal) across 13 reference conditions
(Supplementary Table 16): dorsolateral prefrontal cortex medial ganglionic eminence interneuron, bone marrow
(hematopoietic stem cell, megakaryocyte–erythroid progenitor, proerythroblast, erythroblast, orthochromatic
erythroblast, and granulocyte–monocyte progenitor), interventricular septum mast cell, liver neutrophil, lung
monocyte, mucosa dendritic cell, thymus pro-B cell, and blood naive B cell. Links were plotted in 500-bp midpoint
bins (maximum gradient score per bin; Fig. 4h) alongside GRCh38 reference intervals (converted from GRCh37
via UCSC liftOver where reported on hg19) for the erythroid +62 kb (chr2:60490400-60491800), +58 kb core
(chr2:60495049-60495539, intersection of Bauer et al. and Javierre et al.), and +55 kb (chr2:60497800-
60499200) enhancers57,58,60, and promoter capture Hi-C HindIII fragments in naive and total B cells (+200 kb,
chr2:60352101-6035367558) and dorsolateral prefrontal cortex (+479 kb, chr2:60071284-6007709659).
For Fig. 4i, bone marrow erythroblast base-resolution scATAC-seq predictions (10x nuclear channel, 128-bp
centered moving average) and active E2G links were paired with wild-type and sg1617-edited HUDEP-2 data60:
unsmoothed 20-bp ATAC-seq bigWig tracks (GSE292669; mean of two coverage-normalized replicates) and

GWAS analysis
GWAS fine-mapping data and trait definitions
We obtained fine-mapped GWAS credible sets and study metadata from the Open Targets Platform (release
26.03)27. We used credible sets fine-mapped with SuSiE (with an in-sample or out-of-sample LD reference),
excluding credible sets derived with PICS. We grouped GWAS studies into 28 traits by curating their reported trait
names (Supplementary Table 17). Two of these traits pooled related diseases: autoimmune diseases (inflammatory
bowel disease, rheumatoid arthritis, psoriasis and psoriatic arthritis, systemic lupus erythematosus, type 1 diabetes,
autoimmune thyroid disease, ankylosing spondylitis, multiple sclerosis, celiac disease, myasthenia gravis, Sjögren’s
syndrome and sarcoidosis) and asthma/allergy (asthma, eczema, urticaria and allergic rhinitis). For each trait, we
pooled the credible sets of all its studies and assigned each variant its maximum PIP across them. All analyses
used SNVs only. As a background set, we used common SNVs (gnomAD72 v4.1 genome allele frequency between
0.05 and 0.95) that were not in any of the SuSiE GWAS credible sets. The combined set of variants corresponds to
the GWAS Panel, excluding the variants unique to Traitgym.
Definition of called variants
For the GWAS analysis presented in this paper, we restricted our attention to AG2 single-cell gene expression
variant effect predictions, as these can be directly used to nominate genes and cell types. Specifically, for each
variant, nearby protein-coding gene (GENCODE v46132) and condition, we recorded the predicted log fold change
in single-cell gene expression (Variant scoring), as well as the gene’s predicted expression level for whichever
allele was more highly expressed. We say AG2 called a variant–gene pair in a condition if, in at least one of the
condition’s single-cell gene expression output channels, the absolute predicted log fold change |∆expr| exceeded
0.1 and the gene was predicted to be expressed (log(predicted CPM + 1) > 1.5). When several channels qualified,
we kept the one with the largest absolute predicted log fold change. For the six conditions scored using expression
conditioning, a variant–gene pair was also called if the predicted variant effect from AG2’s denoised expression
output passed the same thresholds.
Of the 6,822,130 variants analyzed, 134,813 were called for at least one gene in at least one condition at the
selected thresholds.
TenK10K threshold sweep
We calibrated the two call thresholds (Definition of called variants) using sc-eQTLs from the TenK10K study7. We
used its SuSiE credible sets for protein-coding genes (GENCODE v46132), restricted to SNVs, and its nominal
association 𝑃values for all variant–gene pairs tested in each cell type. We matched each TenK10K cell type to the
AG2 reference condition of the same cell type (Supplementary Table 9). We defined the following variants and
(variant, gene, condition) triplets:
• Fine-mapped eQTLs: SNVs with PIP > 0.9 in at least one credible set (6362 SNVs). Each such credible set
contributed an on-target triplet: the SNV, the gene of the credible set and the reference condition matched to
its cell type (14,010 distinct triplets).
• Off-target triplets: a fine-mapped eQTL paired with a protein-coding gene and cell type for which it was
tested but not associated (nominal 𝑃> 0.05) and not in a credible set. Triplets that were also on-target,
which can occur when two cell types share a reference condition, were excluded.
• Background variants: tested SNVs with a minor allele frequency >0.01 that had nominal 𝑃> 0.05 for
every gene and cell type tested and were not in any credible set (1,235,667 SNVs).
All other SNVs, including credible-set SNVs with PIP ≤0.9, were excluded.
We scored these variants using the AG2 single-cell gene expression output and performed a sweep over the
thresholds we used to define a call. Specifically, we varied the log fold change threshold from 0.05 to 0.20 in steps
of 0.01, and the expression threshold over 0 (no expression requirement), 0.5, 1, 1.5, 1.75, 2 and 2.5. For each of
the 112 threshold combinations, we computed three quantities:
48
.
CC-BY-NC 4.0 International license
perpetuity. It is made available under a 
preprint (which was not certified by peer review) is the author/funder, who has granted bioRxiv a license to display the preprint in 
The copyright holder for this
this version posted November 17, 2026. 
; 
https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.11.11.765857
doi: 
bioRxiv preprint 


• Recall: the fraction of fine-mapped eQTLs called in at least one of their on-target triplets.
• Enrichment for fine-mapped eQTLs: the recall divided by the fraction of background variants called for any
gene in any of the 26 reference conditions. This comparison is conservative, because fine-mapped eQTLs
counted only if called on target.
• On-target enrichment: the fraction of on-target triplets called divided by the fraction of off-target triplets
called.
Cell-type specificity of calls
To relate TSS distance to cell-type specificity without relying on tissue or dataset labels, we grouped reference
conditions by transcriptional identity. Starting from the CPM matrix (averaged over channels where relevant) of
the 1943 reference conditions in the AG2 single-cell expression pre-training corpus, we retained protein-coding
genes with mean expression ≥1 in at least 20 conditions, applied a log(CPM + 1) transform, and selected the
2000 genes with the highest standard deviation across conditions. Each gene was scaled to unit variance without
centering, flooring standard deviations at their median.
We factorized this matrix using non-negative matrix factorization (NMF; scikit-learn127; 40 programs, NNDSVDa
initialization, Frobenius loss), weighting each condition’s program usages by the ℓ2 norm of the program’s gene
loadings and normalizing them to sum to one. We excluded eight programs manually judged to reflect technical
factors rather than cell-type identity and renormalized over the remaining 32 programs.
We clustered the 504 GWAS-panel reference conditions by Ward’s hierarchical clustering on the square root of
their program usages, cutting the tree into 60 cell-type clusters (grouping the same cell type across tissues and
datasets; median size, 8 conditions) nested within 12 lineage clusters. Each condition scored with expression
conditioning formed a singleton cell-type cluster within the lineage cluster of a related reference condition: decidual
stromal cells for both endometrial stromal cell states, tracheal chondrocytes for osteoblasts and preosteoblasts,
splenic macrophages for osteoclasts, and bone-marrow erythroblasts for megakaryocytes.
A call in a given condition was considered corroborated if the condition was scored using expression conditioning
or if another condition in its cell-type cluster showed a same-sign effect at relaxed thresholds (|log fold change| >
0.05 and log(predicted CPM + 1) > 1, in any track). Call breadth was defined as the number of lineage clusters
containing at least one corroborated called condition (or any called condition, if none were corroborated), yielding
four specificity categories:
• Single condition: called in 1 condition.
• Lineage-restricted: called in ≥2 conditions, breadth 1.
• Multi-lineage: breadth 2–3.
• Broad: breadth ≥4.
Call-level analyses (variant-to-gene TSS distance) used each call’s own category, whereas variant-level
analyses (e.g., ENCODE cCRE overlap) assigned each called variant the broadest category among its calls. Here
and below, a gene’s TSS was taken from its MANE Select transcript131 in GENCODE v46, or from its Ensembl
canonical transcript for the 839 protein-coding genes lacking one.
Conservation of called variants
We annotated each variant with its distance to the nearest protein-coding TSS, in four bins: <1 kb, 1–10 kb,
10–100 kb and >100 kb. Baseline conservation falls steeply with distance to the TSS, so we compared called
with uncalled variants within each distance bin. We extracted phyloP scores at each variant’s position from the
Zoonomia 241-mammal alignment133. A position was conserved if phyloP ≥2.27, the threshold for evolutionarily
constrained bases at a 5% false discovery rate in the Zoonomia alignment133. Variants without a phyloP score
were excluded.
Overlap of called variants with ENCODE cCREs
We intersected variants with the ENCODE registry of cCREs, version 440. A variant was in a cCRE if its position lay
within any cCRE. We stratified called variants by their variant-level specificity category. For each category and
distance bin, we computed the fraction of called variants in a cCRE, with 95% CIs from the block bootstrap, and
compared it with the fraction of uncalled variants in a cCRE in the same distance bin.
Cell-type enrichment of GWAS credible sets
For each trait and condition, we tested whether the trait’s credible-set SNVs were called in the condition more
often than background variants. Let 𝑎be the number of the trait’s credible-set SNVs called (for any gene) in the
49
.

condition, and 𝑏the corresponding number of background variants. Let 𝑁CS and 𝑁BG be the total numbers of
credible-set SNVs of the trait and of background variants. We applied a one-sided Fisher’s exact test for enrichment
to the 2 × 2 table [[𝑎, 𝑏], [𝑁CS −𝑎, 𝑁BG −𝑏]] and corrected for the number of conditions tested per trait with the
Benjamini–Hochberg procedure. We refer to the corrected 𝑃value as the enrichment FDR. For each trait, the
condition with the lowest enrichment FDR was its trait-matched condition.
Seventeen traits had an enrichment FDR ≤10−7 in at least one condition: Alzheimer’s disease, asthma/allergy,
atrial fibrillation, autoimmune diseases, blood pressure, cholesterol, coronary artery disease, glomerular filtration
rate, height, lung capacity, platelet count/volume, prostate cancer, red blood cell count/volume, serum globulin, type
2 diabetes, waist-to-hip ratio and white blood cell count. These traits and their trait-matched condition were used in
all subsequent analyses. The ontologies of the trait-matched conditions are given in (Supplementary Table 18).
To construct the trait-by-cell-type heatmap, we transformed the enrichment FDRs of these 17 traits to
−log10(FDR) and 𝑧-scored each trait across all conditions. For each trait, we took the five conditions with
the lowest enrichment FDR (among those with FDR < 0.05) and displayed the union of these conditions across
traits. Traits and conditions were ordered by hierarchical clustering of the displayed 𝑧-scores (average linkage,
Euclidean distance). Column annotations show broad lineages, assigned by matching condition names against
curated lists of cell-type keywords.
PIP enrichment
For each of the 17 traits, we considered all of its credible-set SNVs, with one entry per trait–SNV pair carrying the
SNV’s maximum PIP across the trait’s credible sets. We binned the pairs by PIP: 0–0.01, 0.01–0.05, 0.05–0.1,
0.1–0.25, 0.25–0.5, 0.5–0.75 and 0.75–1 (intervals closed on the right). We used two definitions of a called
trait–SNV pair. Under the first, the SNV was called (for any gene) in the trait’s trait-matched condition. Under the
second, used for comparison, the SNV was called in any of the 510 conditions. For each definition, we pooled pairs
across traits and computed the call rate (the fraction of called pairs) in each PIP bin relative to the call rate in the
lowest bin (PIP ≤0.01), with 95% CIs from the block bootstrap.
PIP enrichment stratified by eQTL colocalization
This analysis used the same trait–SNV pairs, trait-matched conditions, PIP bins and fold definition as the PIP
enrichment analysis. We split the trait–SNV pairs into two groups according to whether any of the trait’s credible
sets containing the SNV colocalized with an eQTL or sc-eQTL credible set, using the colocalization results of the
Open Targets Platform (release 26.03, the same release as the GWAS data)27. Colocalization was defined as a
COLOC134 posterior probability of a shared causal variant (𝐻4) ≥0.8, for any gene, tissue or cell type. Folds were
computed separately within each group, relative to the call rate at PIP ≤0.01 in the same group, with 95% CIs
from the block bootstrap.
Direction-of-effect concordance
If a gene affects a trait through its expression level, the ALT allele’s effect on the trait should equal the direction of
the predicted expression change multiplied by the effect of increased gene dosage. We approximated the effect of
increased dosage as the opposite of the effect of loss of function (LoF), as estimated by rare-variant gene burden
tests. We obtained the burden test results from the Open Targets Platform (release 26.03, the same release as the
GWAS data)27.
We tested this prediction for every trait–gene pair in which the gene was called at one of the trait’s credible-set
SNVs in the trait-matched condition, counting each pair once. Each pair was represented by the highest-PIP SNV
among those at which the gene was called (PIP: the maximum across the trait’s credible sets; ties broken by the
largest absolute predicted log fold change) and by the sign of that SNV’s AG2 score. Directions were then defined
as follows:
• GWAS direction: the sign of the SNV’s ALT-allele effect size in the trait’s credible sets that contained it. Each
credible set contributed under every ontology term of its study, and for each term we took the majority sign
across credible sets (ties were discarded).
• LoF direction: the sign of the effect estimate (or the log odds ratio for binary traits) of the most significant
Open Targets gene burden result for the same gene and term. Burden results for synonymous-variant masks
were excluded, because these masks serve as negative controls. A positive sign means that LoF increases
the trait value or disease risk.
Terms were matched by name, so that the same trait under different ontologies (EFO and the Ontology of
Biological Attributes) matched. Medication-use, sex-interaction and Gene Ontology terms were excluded. A term
was concordant if sign(GWAS effect) = −sign(AG2 score) × sign(LoF effect).

.

Each trait–gene pair was classified as concordant or discordant by majority over its matched terms, that is,
terms with both a GWAS and a LoF direction. Pairs without a matched term, as well as ties, were excluded. We
computed the fraction of concordant pairs among concordant and discordant pairs and tested it against 0.5 with a
two-sided exact binomial test.
We repeated the test on the pairs whose gene was not the nearest gene to the pair’s SNV, that is, pairs in which
another protein-coding gene had a TSS strictly closer to the SNV than the gene’s own TSS. Ties, which occur for
example when a readthrough gene shares the TSS of its upstream gene, were counted as nearest.
Locus-level and metacell variant interpretation
Individual GWAS loci (Fig. 5a,b,l; Supplementary Fig. 15a,b) were scored with the all-folds distilled AG2 model
using the single-cell gene expression variant scorer (Variant scoring; observed channel with the largest absolute
predicted log fold change retained when multiple channels applied):
• ETS2 (21q22): All seven SNVs in the inflammatory bowel disease credible set of Stankey et al.67 containing
rs2836882 (chr21:39094644:G>A) were scored on nearby protein-coding genes across seven primary
ileum immune reference conditions (Fig. 5a,b, Supplementary Table 19).
• SCUBE3 (6p21.31): The two SNVs in the standing-height credible set containing rs2038740 (chr6:
35146765:C>T) were scored in adult bronchus chondrocytes (UBERON:0002185, CL:0000138). Satu-
rated ISM of predicted SCUBE3 expression (10x 5’ whole-cell channel) was computed on the alternate
(rs2038740-T) sequence across a 64-bp window and displayed across the central 42 bp (chr6:35146733-
35146775; Fig. 5l).
• HERC2–OCA2 (15q13.1): In primary skin melanocytes (UBERON:0002097, CL:0000148), we scored
rs12913832 (chr15:28120472:A>G), rs6497271 (chr15:28120285:A>G), the 26 remaining SNVs in
the adjacent melanoma credible set135, and the double-alternate haplotype carrying both rs12913832-G and
rs6497271-G relative to the reference sequence (Supplementary Fig. 15a).
• SMIM1 (1p36.32): The two reticulocyte-count credible-set variants rs6673829 (chr1:3774926:G>A) and
rs1175550 (chr1:3774964:A>G)27,136 were scored on SMIM1 across 21 primary bone-marrow hematopoi-
etic reference conditions (Supplementary Fig. 15b).
To resolve variant effects across developmental and tissue single-cell atlases (Fig. 5m, Supplementary Fig. 14d,
Supplementary Fig. 15c,d), raw UMI count matrices from CZ CELLxGENE Discover were randomly subsampled
without replacement within each cell type to at most 10,000 cells (seed 0, excluding zero-count cells) and mapped
to the cell encoder’s GENCODE v46 Ensembl vocabulary (explicitly stored zeros removed). All retained cells
were passed through the cell encoder (unmasked all-folds model, 𝑧∈R256) and partitioned within each cell
type into 𝑘= max(1, round(𝑛/64)) cell-type-pure metacells by 𝑘-means clustering (random_state = 1, n_
init = 10). Each metacell was scored with the all-folds post-trained AG2 denoised single-cell gene expression
scorer (Stage 4: Post-training for expression conditioning), conditioned on the mean cell embedding of its
member cells (for metacells exceeding 256 cells, the 256 cells nearest the metacell mean cell embedding
in Euclidean distance were averaged). Two-dimensional UMAP coordinates (umap-learn50, min_dist = 0.5,
random_state = 42) were fitted on single-cell embeddings, with metacell mean cell embeddings projected
via transform. This protocol was applied to: (i) human embryonic limb lateral plate mesoderm (5–9 post-
conception weeks; CELLxGENE f9685456-c8a8-43ad-a326-476273ef36a0; 100,935 cells across 34 cell
types, 1575 metacells)68 for rs2038740 on SCUBE3, alongside metacell pseudobulk log(CPM + 1) expression
of SCUBE3 and SOX9 (Fig. 5m, Supplementary Fig. 14d); (ii) normal-donor human distal airways (CELLxGENE
b61a921b-7fa3-4b42-b455-aaaf32447920; 69,830 cells across 48 author clusters grouped into 19 cell
types, 1088 metacells)137 for rs35705950 (chr11:1219991:G>T) on MUC5B (Supplementary Fig. 15c); and
(iii) human endometrium (CELLxGENE 9ddea8d9-cc4c-420a-90f6-880996f808d4; 75,844 cells across 16
cell types, 1183 metacells)130 for rs3820282 (chr1:22141722:C>T) on WNT4 (Supplementary Fig. 15d).
Statistics
Unless stated otherwise, 95% CIs of rates and fold enrichments came from a genomic block bootstrap: the genome
was divided into 1-Mb blocks, and each of the 2000 replicates resampled the blocks with replacement and kept all
entries (variants or trait–SNV pairs) of each drawn block, across groups and traits. CIs are the 2.5th and 97.5th
percentiles of the replicate statistic. This approach accounts for correlation between nearby variants (for example,
through LD or shared regulatory elements) and for loci shared by related traits. Fold enrichments are ratios of rates
pooled across traits. Fisher’s exact and binomial tests were computed with SciPy138.

Code availability
