d/alphagenome, which includes Python software to interact with the model. AlphaGenome2 model weights,
code, and precomputed predictions are intended for research purposes only. They contain predictions with varying
levels of confidence and caution should be exercised in their use. The information provided is not intended to be
a substitute for professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment, and does not constitute medical or other
professional advice. AlphaGenome2 and its outputs have not been validated for, and are not approved for, any
clinical or diagnostic use. The model source code and weights will be provided upon final publication.
AI use declaration
Large language models including Gemini 4 Argon, accessed with Google Antigravity (an agentic development
environment) were used throughout this project to brainstorm, critique and implement model analyses and
evaluation designs. Gemini was also used to draft and edit manuscript prose and visualization code. In each
of these steps, the authors steered the agents and ran several rounds of adversarial review. Every AI-proposed
hypothesis, analysis script, quantitative result, figure, and manuscript passage was inspected, verified against
primary data, and revised by the authors, and all bibliographic citations were checked against original sources. The
authors take full responsibility for all analyses, claims, and content in this manuscript.

Supplementary Figure 1 | Overview of the AlphaGenome 2 training data corpus. (a,b) Modality coverage across conditions for human
(a) and mouse (b). Horizontal bars (left) give the total number of conditions with observed data for each modality; vertical bars (top) give the
number of conditions observed with each combination of modalities, indicated by connected dots below, for the most frequent combinations.
(c,d) Tissue coverage of the single-cell data for human (c) and mouse (d). Left, number of cells per tissue (log scale); right, number of annotated
cell types per tissue. Bars are colored by developmental stage of the source samples (adult, developmental or mixed). (e) Number of single-cell
studies per organism, split by whether a study contributed both scRNA-seq and scATAC-seq, scRNA-seq only or scATAC-seq only.

Supplementary Figure 2 | Benchmark comparison of AlphaGenome 2 versus AlphaGenome 1 across core splicing, chromatin, and
polyadenylation variant tasks. (a) Splicing QTL (sQTL) causality prediction. Tissue-weighted mean area under the precision–recall curve
(AUPRC) comparing AlphaGenome 1 (AG1) and AlphaGenome 2 (AG2) across five variant scoring heads (RNA-seq coverage, splice sites,
splice site usage, splice junction, and composite “SJ+SSU+SS”) for proximal (≤200 bp, left) and distal (≤10 kb, right) fine-mapped GTEx sQTLs.
Dashed horizontal lines denote random baseline performance. (b) Splicing outlier causality prediction. Comparison of AUPRC between AG1
and AG2 across splicing and RNA coverage variant scorers for distinguishing rare GTEx splicing-outlier-associated variants from matched
controls. (c) Chromatin QTL effect size prediction. Bar plot comparing Pearson correlation (𝑟) between predicted and observed variant effect
sizes across chromatin accessibility and DNase hypersensitivity QTL benchmarks (“caQTL African”, “caQTL European”, “dsQTL Yoruba”,
“caQTL SMC” in smooth muscle cells, and “caQTL Microglia”) for AG1 and AG2. (d) Chromatin QTL causality prediction. Comparison of AUPRC
between AG1 and AG2 for distinguishing putatively causal chromatin QTLs from matched negative controls across the “caQTL African”, “caQTL
European”, and “dsQTL Yoruba” callsets. Dashed horizontal lines indicate random baseline AUPRC. (e) Polyadenylation QTL (paQTL) causality
prediction. Violin plots showing AUPRC distributions across 𝑛= 100 random negative-matching permutations (Methods) for AG1 and AG2 on
fine-mapped GTEx paQTLs stratified by maximum distance threshold to the target polyadenylation site (from 50 bp to 10,000 bp; x-axis labels
give the number of positive paQTLs per threshold). Violins span the observed data range; dashed horizontal lines inside each violin indicate the
median and the 25th and 75th percentiles.

Supplementary Figure 4 | Architectural ablation of biological conditioning pathways in AlphaGenome 2. (a) Schematic of the conditioning
pathways evaluated across network stages. Species and biological condition embeddings are summed and injected via FiLM across network
modules: standard AG2 (“Full conditioning”) modulates the sequence encoder (stem and 6 downsampling convolutional block stages),
central transformer tower (128 bp resolution), and sequence decoder (7 upsampling convolutional block stages), whereas restricted ablation
configurations selectively enable FiLM in subsets of these stages or in the pointwise output embedder. (b) Impact of conditioning placement
on held-out genome prediction accuracy. Bar plots comparing model configurations where biological conditioning is restricted to specific
architectural stages (“Multitask” baseline without conditioning, “Output only”, “Decoder only”, “Decoder & output”, “Transformer & decoder”,
“Transformer, decoder & output”, and “Full conditioning”). Note that “Decoder only” does not condition assays like ChIP-seq defined only at
128-bp resolution. Top row: Cross-condition gene expression log fold change (LFC) Pearson correlation (𝑟) across ENCODE cell lines (left) and
GTEx tissues (right). Bottom row: Consensus peak classification area under the precision–recall curve (AUPRC) across ChIP-seq histone
modifications (left) and ChIP-seq transcription factors (right). Dots indicate individual replicate training runs (𝑛= 3 seeds per configuration).

Supplementary Figure 7 | Cross-modality bulk epigenomic imputation and primary-cell enhancer annotation with AlphaGenome 2.
(a) Bulk epigenomic imputation dataset split. Overview of training, validation, and held-out test tracks across 32 human conditions with
held-out tracks (tissues, cell lines, and primary cell types) spanning four bulk assay categories: transcription factor (TF) ChIP-seq (“ChIP TF”),
histone modification ChIP-seq (“ChIP Histone”), chromatin accessibility (“Accessibility (DNASE/ATAC)”), and gene expression (“Expression
(RNA/CAGE)”). Right bar plot shows track counts per condition, stacked by split. (b) Average imputation performance across modalities
comparing AG2, retrained Avocado tensor factorization, cross-condition channel mean (“Mean”), and five-closest training-track oracle (“Closest”).
Left four groups: support-weighted consensus peak area under the precision–recall curve (AUPRC) for ATAC-seq, DNase-seq, histone ChIP-seq,
and TF ChIP-seq. Far right: cross-condition gene log fold change Pearson 𝑟(“Gene LFC Pearson”) for bulk RNA-seq. (c) Example imputed
H3K4me1 profile (held-out track) at the LMO1 locus in SK-N-SH neuroblastoma cells. Tracks show consensus peak empirical frequency
bins (0% to 100%, top color bar), experimentally called peaks (“Peak call”, ticks), and observed versus predicted H3K4me1 coverage for
AG2, “Mean”, and Avocado. (d) Classification of open non-promoter representative DNase I hypersensitive sites (rDHSs) with enhancer-like
signatures. Bar plot comparing AUPRC across nine primary peripheral blood mononuclear cell (PBMC) types (CD8 TCM, B Mem, CD8
TEM, B cell, B Naive, CD14 Monocyte, CD8 T Naive, CD4 T Naive, and NK) using AG2-imputed H3K27ac (“Imputation”), observed H3K27ac
ChIP-seq from a lineage-matched cell line (“Matched Cell Line ChIP (Observed)”), and observed scATAC-seq alone (“ATAC (Observed)”).
Dashed horizontal segments indicate random baseline prevalence per cell type. (e) Cell-type specificity of H3K27ac imputation. Hexbin density
scatterplot comparing predicted versus observed differences from the cross-cell-type mean H3K27ac 𝑧-score across genome-wide rDHSs in
the nine PBMC types (Pearson 𝑟= 0.44; Methods).

Supplementary Figure 8 | Track-level and frequency-stratified bulk epigenomic imputation benchmarks with AlphaGenome 2. (a) Track-
level imputation comparison. Scatterplot comparing per-track imputation accuracy (consensus peak area under the precision–recall curve
(AUPRC) for chromatin assays; cross-condition gene log fold change Pearson 𝑟for RNA-seq) between AG2 (x-axis) and retrained Avocado
(y-axis) across all 181 evaluated test tracks (RNA-seq, 𝑛= 5; chromatin accessibility, 𝑛= 17; histone ChIP-seq, 𝑛= 93; transcription factor (TF)
ChIP-seq, 𝑛= 66; Methods). Points below the dashed diagonal (𝑦= 𝑥) indicate tracks where AG2 outperforms Avocado (166 of 181 tracks).
(b–e) Consensus peak classification AUPRC stratified by condition specificity. Support-weighted peak AUPRC across eight empirical peak
frequency bins (proportion of profiled conditions in which a consensus peak is called active, in 12.5% increments from rare condition-specific
elements, 0–12.5%, to ubiquitous elements, 87.5–100%) comparing AG2, Avocado, cross-condition channel mean (“Mean”), and five-closest
training-track oracle (“Closest”) across (b) TF ChIP-seq, (c) DNase-seq, (d) ATAC-seq, and (e) histone ChIP-seq. Dashed horizontal segments
indicate empirical random baseline prevalence within each frequency bin. (f) Continuous profile correlation across chromatin modalities. Bar
plot comparing mean genome-wide continuous Pearson 𝑟at 32 bp resolution across held-out test tracks for ATAC-seq, DNase-seq, histone
ChIP-seq, and TF ChIP-seq across AG2, Avocado, “Mean”, and “Closest”.

Supplementary Figure 9 | Evaluation of the single-cell expression autoencoder and post-trained AlphaGenome 2. (a) Single-cell
integration benchmarking (scIB) of cell embeddings on the CITE-seq RNA component of a SARS-CoV-2 vaccination PBMC dataset1. The AG2
cell encoder with no dataset-specific training or batch covariates is compared with an scVI model trained on this dataset with donor as the
batch covariate. Bars show the composite biological conservation, batch correction and total scores from scib-metrics2. (b) Uniform manifold
approximation and projection (UMAP) of AG2 cell encoder embeddings (left) and scVI latent representations (right) for the same cells, colored
by level-2 cell type. (c) Recovery of cell-type-specific expression by autoencoder reconstructions across 16 held-out CELLxGENE test-split
datasets. For each cell type, cell embeddings were averaged over 𝑘randomly sampled cells (𝑘= 1 to 64, or all cells; 20 trials) and decoded into
a denoised profile, which was compared with the observed pseudobulk by cross-gene LFC Pearson 𝑟across cell types. Individual curves show
each dataset alongside the median across datasets. (d,e) Gene LFC Pearson 𝑟for AG2 post-trained from a single pre-trained model prior
to condition refinement (y-axis) versus from the distilled student model (x-axis), across evaluation groups from CELLxGENE training-split (d;
𝑛= 147) and test-split (e; 𝑛= 20). Dashed line, 𝑦= 𝑥.

Supplementary Figure 10 | Evaluation of post-trained AlphaGenome 2. (a,b) Cell-type-pooled TenK10K single-cell expression QTL
(sc-eQTL) sign accuracy versus recall in a and causality precision versus recall in b across the 25 cell types matched in an independent
PBMC dataset3,4. Curves compare expression conditioning on mean cell embeddings from that dataset with reference conditioning on
matched reference conditions. Legend values give area under the curve (AUC) in a and area under the precision–recall curve (AUPRC) in b.
(c,d) Cell-type-pooled IBDverse sc-eQTL sign accuracy versus recall in c and causality precision versus recall in d across the 47 intestinal cell
types5. Curves compare expression conditioning on mean cell embeddings from each cell type’s donor-majority intestinal tissue in the IBDverse
dataset with reference conditioning on matched reference conditions. Legend values give AUC in c and AUPRC in d. (e,f) Cell-type-weighted
TenK10K sc-eQTL sign AUROC in e and causality AUPRC in f for post-trained AG2 conditioned on cell embeddings averaged over 1, 16 or 128
randomly sampled cells per cell type3,4. Bars show the mean across 10 random trials and points show individual trials. Dashed lines indicate
conditioning on all cells. (g,h) Sign accuracy versus recall pooled across cell types and brain regions in the Parkinson’s disease cohort6 for
variants with both chromatin accessibility QTL and allele-specific chromatin accessibility effects in g and for overlapping expression QTLs in h.
Curves compare expression conditioning on mean cell embeddings for each cell type and brain region with reference conditioning on matched
reference conditions. Legend values give AUC. (i) Predicted effect of rs13066062 on CXCR6 and FYCO1 expression for post-trained AG2
conditioned on the mean cell embeddings of naive CD8+ T (CD8 Naive), effector memory CD8+ T (CD8 TEM) and vaccine-induced group A
CD8+ T (VI-A CD8+ T) cells from the SARS-CoV-2 vaccination dataset1. (j) Predicted change in CXCR6 expression caused by rs13066062
versus observed CXCR6 expression (log(CPM + 1)) for 881 metacells from the same dataset (Spearman 𝜌= −0.25). Points indicate VI-A
CD8+ T, other T-cell and remaining metacells. (k) Imputed histone ChIP-seq profiles and variant effects at rs13066062 for the three CD8+ T-cell
states in i (Methods). Left: imputed H3K27ac signal across a 10-kb window at FYCO1, centered on rs13066062 (chr3:45976852:G>A;
orange line). Right: predicted variant scores across H3K27ac, H3K4me1, H3K4me3, H3K36me3, H3K27me3 and H3K9me3.

Supplementary Figure 11 | Extended evaluation and ablations of enhancer–gene linking across CRISPRi benchmarks and primary
brain cell types. (a–c) Comparison of unnormalized (solid bars) versus window-normalized (hatched bars; divided by the mean absolute
attribution across the input window) gradient attribution scores on the ENCODE-rE2G K562 CRISPRi benchmark (a)7, the held-out ENCODE-
rE2G CRISPRi benchmark across five cell lines (b)7, and the human astrocyte CRISPRi benchmark (c)8 for AG2, AG19, and the four-replicate
Borzoi ensemble10. (d,e) Comparison of reference-conditioned AG2 (solid bars) and post-trained AG2 using expression conditioning on
mean cell embeddings from the corresponding scRNA-seq dataset (hatched bars) on the ENCODE-rE2G K562 benchmark (d)7 and the
human astrocyte benchmark (e)8, alongside AG1, the Borzoi ensemble, and ENCODE-rE2G extended and ENCODE-rE2G (d) or EGrf (e).
(f) Enhancer–gene (E2G) linking performance on the full ENCODE-rE2G K562 CRISPRi benchmark7, comparing AG2, AG1, the Borzoi
ensemble, ENCODE-rE2G extended, and ENCODE-rE2G. In (a–f), bars show area under the precision–recall curve (AUPRC) across all tested
element–gene pairs and stratified by enhancer distance to the target gene transcription start site (TSS; 0–10 kb, 10–100 kb, and 100–2500 kb);
horizontal dotted lines indicate the inverse TSS distance baseline. (g) Pairwise Jaccard similarity of predicted E2G links for protein-coding
genes at caQTL-ASCA peaks in the cingulate cortex of the Parkinson’s disease cohort6, comparing post-trained AG2 conditioned on mean cell
embeddings for each cell type (rows) with AG2 conditioned on the corresponding reference condition proxies from the training corpus (columns;
left), and comparing the reference condition proxies with one another across the same layout (right).

centage of genes retained in K562 as a function of the predicted log-transformed gene expression threshold across CRISPRi positive-hit target

indicates the selected target gene expression threshold (^𝑦𝑔≥0.5; Methods). (b) Percentage of candidate enhancer tiles retained in K562

K562); dashed vertical line indicates the selected accessibility threshold (scATAC ≥1.25). (c) Precision, recall, and 𝐹1 score across gradient
attribution score thresholds on the K562 ENCODE CRISPRi enhancer–gene (E2G) benchmark (with each CRISPRi element–gene pair mapped
to its best-overlapping 500-bp atlas tile), shown before and after applying the target gene expression and local chromatin accessibility gates;
dashed vertical line indicates the selected gradient attribution threshold (score ≥0.0012). (d–f) Same as (a–c), evaluated on the astrocytes
CRISPRi E2G benchmark.

Supplementary Note 1 | In silico study of individual GWAS loci
Beyond large-scale analyses, AG2 can also be used to study individual loci in silico. For example, the HERC2–
OCA2 locus contains two adjacent credible sets associated with melanoma15. The first is fine-mapped to a single
variant, rs12913832. This variant is a major determinant of eye and skin pigmentation in individuals of European
ancestry20. It reduces OCA2 expression by weakening a chromatin loop between an enhancer and the OCA2
promoter21. The second locus contains 27 variants, none with a posterior inclusion probability (PIP) above 0.15.
This includes rs6497271, located 187 bp from rs12913832. In melanocytes, AG2 predicts that both rs12913832
and rs6497271 reduce expression of the pigmentation gene OCA2, with little or no effect predicted for the other
variants in linkage disequilibrium (Supplementary Fig. 15a). This agrees with massively parallel reporter assay,
chromatin interaction and CRISPR interference experiments that identified rs6497271 as a functional variant in
the same OCA2 enhancer22. Carrying both derived alleles is common in individuals of European ancestry. AG2
predicts that the effect of this haplotype is approximately the sum of the two individual effects, in line with its lowest
enhancer activity in reporter assays22.
By contrast, at the SMIM1 locus, AG2 predicts opposite effects in erythroblasts for two variants only 38 bp apart
(Supplementary Fig. 15b). Each variant is fine-mapped (PIP > 0.99) in a separate credible set for reticulocyte
count16,17. The associations match these predictions: rs1175550 is predicted to increase SMIM1 expression and
is associated with increased reticulocyte count, whereas rs6673829 is predicted to decrease expression and is
associated with decreased reticulocyte count. We anticipate that AG2 will help to identify further loci with multiple
causal variants and to derive allelic series.
AG2 can also resolve the cell type in which a variant acts. The pulmonary fibrosis risk variant rs35705950 is
known from bulk lung tissue to increase MUC5B expression23. MUC5B is expressed mainly by airway secretory
cells, whereas pulmonary fibrosis primarily affects the distal lung and alveolar epithelium. We applied AG2 to
a single-cell atlas of the human distal airways18. AG2 predicts that the largest effect of the variant is activation
of MUC5B in alveolar type 2 (AT2) cells, which express the gene at very low levels at baseline (Supplementary
Fig. 15c). Experimental evidence supports this prediction. MUC5B is co-expressed with the AT2 marker SFTPC in
the lungs of individuals with idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis. Moreover, Muc5b overexpression in the distal airways
and alveoli of mice exacerbates bleomycin-induced lung fibrosis24.
Similarly, rs3820282 is associated with endometriosis, uterine leiomyoma and gestational duration25,26. In an
endometrial cell atlas19, AG2 predicts that this variant increases WNT4 expression, most strongly in endometrial
stromal cells. This agrees with mice in which the alternative allele was introduced by CRISPR–Cas9, leading to
upregulation of transcription of Wnt4 in endometrial stroma25 (Supplementary Fig. 15d).
